# 04 · 50 Complete Filming Scripts

Ordered by video ID, which is production order (grouped by location). `{{REAL: ...}}` marks a fact only Alex knows; fill it with the true value or cut the line.

## V001 · "Walk us through your creative process" (the Notes app reveal)

**Rank 1 of 50 · Score 90/100 · Day 1 · Batch A: Desk and whiteboard, solo · Business comedy and skits · 9s**

### Reference
- Instagram · @onedentalbh · https://www.instagram.com/reel/DfqcP9lv7dF
- Posted 2026-11-13 · 19.5M views · 3378.7x the creator's median · creator had 2,300 followers · checked 2026-11-16 (vidIQ outlier index)
- Footage reviewed: yes · Not available: likes (not returned by the index), comments (not returned by the index), shares (not returned by the index), metrics_source_url (view count comes from the vidIQ index, not a public page)

### Why the reference worked
- **Observed hook:** No speech. On-screen text: "“Servers are down we lost all our data”" (top) and "Me on my first day at Microsoft" (bottom).
- **First frame:** A man stands at a computer station in a clinical scanning room, hands on keyboard and mouse, looking nervous.
- **Structure:** Two static text lines set a high-stakes premise for 8 seconds. He holds a nervous look, then one camera pan lands on the monitor, which shows the visual punchline. No cuts, no speech, music only.
- **Mechanism:** The text makes a serious claim, the face sells it, and the pan to a screen replaces the claim with something small and harmless. The viewer has to read the screen to get the joke, which forces a pause or a rewatch on an 8-second clip.
- **Observed evidence:** 19.5M views on an account with 2.3K followers and a 5.8K median (3378.7x). 8.54 seconds, 0 cuts, no dialogue, no CTA. Posted 2026-11-13, the day of a Microsoft outage, and the caption uses outage hashtags.
- **Hypothesis (not observed):** most of the spread came from the news day, but the structure (claim, nervous face, pan to a screen that undercuts it) is what made it sendable. Viewers rewatch to read the monitor and send it to coworkers with 'me'. Without a news peg expect a fraction of the views, so the adaptation leans on Alex's own 13.5M proof instead of a brand event.
- **Why it fits Alex:** Same grammar as his two biggest Reels: one shot, no speech, one line of text, under 10 seconds (Central Park 13.5M, travel hack 3.6M). The reveal can point at his own Central Park hit, so a profile visitor immediately finds the pinned video and sees what he does.
- **What is borrowed vs new:** Borrowed: text premise plus nervous hold plus single pan to a screen that deflates the premise. New: the premise is a client asking about his creative process, the screen is his own Notes app, the subject is his real 13.5M Reel, and there is no news event or brand.

### Alex's version
- **Concept:** A client asks the 23-year-old agency owner to explain the creative process behind a 13.5M-view video. The camera pans to his laptop: the entire process is one badly typed note.
- **Audience:** Non-followers 18-34 who make content, freelance, or work in marketing, plus anyone who saw the Central Park Reel. · **Emotion:** Amusement at a confident-looking professional being exposed
- **First frame:** Alex seated at his desk in a collared shirt, hands frozen above the laptop keyboard, eyes wide at the camera. Two text lines already on screen.
- **First spoken line:** None (silent)

| Time | Speaker | Dialogue | Action and acting | Camera | On-screen text | Edit | Audio |
|---|---|---|---|---|---|---|---|
| 0-3s | Alex |  | Sits upright, professional. Eyes locked on camera, lips pressed, the face of someone asked a question on a call he cannot answer. One slow blink. | Phone on tripod, chest-up, laptop edge visible at bottom right of frame. | Top: Client: "Walk us through your creative process" / Bottom: Me, 13.5 million views later: | No cut. Text on from frame 1. | Light staccato strings or a ticking-clock style royalty-free track, starts on frame 1. |
| 3-5s | Alex |  | Tiny nervous swallow. Eyes flick down to the laptop and back to camera as if deciding whether to show it. Very slight head shake. | Same shot, hold. | Same two lines stay up. | No cut. | Music continues. |
| 5-7s | Alex |  | He reaches forward and turns the tripod himself (hand enters frame) so the camera pans from his face to the laptop screen. | Single pan right and slightly down, ends on the laptop screen filling the frame. Lock focus on the screen before filming. | Same two lines stay up, positioned so they do not cover the note. | No cut. If the pan blurs, speed-ramp it slightly rather than cutting. | Music continues. |
| 7-9s | Alex |  | Screen shows the Notes app. One note titled 'ideas'. Body, in lowercase: 'central park but its a parking lot ???' and under it 'ask mom if funny'. Cursor blinking. | Static on screen, text large enough to read on a phone (zoom Notes to 200%). | Same two lines stay up. | End the clip on the screen with no button so it loops back to his face. | Music cuts on the last frame. |

**Alternate hooks**
1. Top: "How long did the 13.5M video take to plan?" / Bottom: Me, a professional:
2. Top: Brand: "Send over the strategy deck" / Bottom: The strategy deck:
3. Top: "You must have a whole team for this" / Bottom: The team:

**Alternate endings**
1. The note has a second line dated the same day: 'update: 13.5M. do not tell client how.'
2. Pan lands on the laptop, which is closed. A sticky note on the lid reads 'central park = parking lot?'. Pan back to his face, he shrugs once.

**Caption:** Three weeks of strategy. #marketing

**CTA:** None. The pinned Central Park Reel is the payoff for anyone who taps the profile.

### Production
- **Location:** Home desk · **People:** 1 · **Film:** 10 min · **Edit:** 8 min · **Difficulty:** easy · **Batchable:** yes
- **Props:** Laptop with Notes app open at 200% zoom, Pre-typed note, Tripod
- **Wardrobe:** Collared shirt, neat hair, the 'on a client call' look
- **Shot list:**
  1. One continuous take: 5 s on face, 2 s pan, 2 s on screen. Shoot 5 takes.
- **Editor:** No cuts. Add both text lines for the full duration, white with black stroke, top line in quotes. Keep the note fully unobstructed in the last 2 seconds. Trim so the last frame is the readable note. Export 9 s.

### Test plan
- **Variants:** Original adaptation; Alternate hook: "You must have a whole team for this" / The team:; Alternate payoff: second note line 'update: 13.5M. do not tell client how.'
- **Brand fit:** 3 of 3 · **Read at:** 24h and 48h · **Primary:** Follows per 1000 non-follower accounts reached
- **Promotion rule:** Brand fit 3: promote if 48h views are at least 3x the trailing median and follows per 1,000 reached or shares per 1,000 reached land in the top quartile; if follows per 1,000 are 2x median at low views, refilm with the 'team' hook.
- **If it wins, make:** Same pan, screen shows a Slack-style message to himself: 'great idea Alex' 'thanks Alex' / "What tools do you use?" pan to a phone with a cracked screen and a roll of tape used as a tripod / "Who approved this concept?" pan to a group chat where the only reply is his mom's thumbs up
- **Why this score:** Matches his proven silent one-shot format and points every viewer at his best Reel. The reference had a news tailwind this version will not have, so the upside is lower than 19.5M suggests, but the cost is 20 minutes.
- **Uncertainties:** The reference's views were tied to a same-day Microsoft outage; the mechanism is untested without a news peg.; The note text must be readable on a small phone; test before posting.; Viewers who have not seen the Central Park Reel still get the joke only if '13.5 million views' reads clearly.

## V002 · Telling people I run a content agency at 23 (four reactions)

**Rank 2 of 50 · Score 87/100 · Day 1 · Batch A: Desk and whiteboard, solo · Entrepreneur lifestyle and storytelling · 7s**

### Reference
- Instagram · @bethonthego_ · https://www.instagram.com/reel/Dej94WNR-QJ
- Posted 2026-10-16 · 5.0M views · 1994.8x the creator's median · creator had 19,000 followers · checked 2026-11-16 (vidIQ outlier index)
- Footage reviewed: yes · Not available: likes: not returned by the index, comments: not returned by the index, shares: not returned by the index, music track: not identified by name with confidence (upbeat pop track)

### Why the reference worked
- **Observed hook:** No speech. On-screen text: "Tell me your generation without telling me your generation / Airport Edition"
- **First frame:** A static shot of an airport gate area with seated passengers and windows onto the tarmac, almost entirely covered by a large color-coded block of text.
- **Structure:** A 5-second static clip under a dense list: a header, then four labeled groups (Boomers, Gen X, Millennials, Gen Z) with 3 to 9 short lines each. There is no sequence. Everything is on screen at once.
- **Mechanism:** More text than can be read in the clip's length. Viewers pause or let it loop several times to finish, find their own group, and comment on whether it is accurate. The longest block belongs to the group most likely to be reading.
- **Observed evidence:** 5.0M views from a 19K-follower account with a 2.5K median, a 1,995x outlier. 0 cuts, no speech, about 110 words on screen for 5 seconds, loops, pop music. The header works as a comment prompt.
- **Hypothesis (not observed):** each loop counts as another play and holds watch time above 100%, which is why a 5-second clip could reach 5M. People send it to someone in a different group with 'this is you'.
- **Why it fits Alex:** Silent, one static shot, under 10 seconds: the form of his top two Reels. The list is built around the sentence that defines him (he runs a content agency at 23), so a non-follower learns who he is by reading the joke.
- **What is borrowed vs new:** Borrowed: a header plus four labeled groups of short lines over a static looping clip, too long to read in one pass. New: the groups are the people who react to his job (his mom, friends with jobs, other agency owners, his landlord), all lines are new, the last group breaks the pattern by repeating one question, and he is visibly in the shot.

### Alex's version
- **Concept:** A 7-second static shot of Alex at his desk on a call, under a dense four-block list of how different people respond when he says what he does. The fourth block is one question asked three ways.
- **Audience:** Freelancers, creators and self-employed people in their twenties, plus their friends with regular jobs. · **Emotion:** Recognition
- **First frame:** Alex at a desk with a laptop and headphones, mid-call, nodding patiently. The full text block covers the upper two thirds of the frame from the first frame.
- **First spoken line:** None (silent)

| Time | Speaker | Dialogue | Action and acting | Camera | On-screen text | Edit | Audio |
|---|---|---|---|---|---|---|---|
| 0-7s | None |  | Alex listens on a call, nods slowly, opens his mouth to answer, gets cut off, closes it, and nods again. Patient face with dead eyes. He repeats the cycle once. | Phone on a tripod, static medium shot from slightly to the side of the desk. | HEADER: "Telling people I run a content agency at 23"  MY MOM "So like a YouTuber?" Sends me job listings. Tells her friends I "do computers."  FRIENDS WITH JOBS "Must be nice." Text me at 2pm on a Tuesday to ask if I'm "busy." Think I wake up at noon. (Sometimes I wake up at noon.)  OTHER AGENCY OWNERS "What's your MRR, bro." Have a course. Are also 23.  MY LANDLORD "Do you have pay stubs?" "Any pay stubs?" "So no pay stubs." | No cuts. All text visible for the full 7 seconds. Each group label in its own color, body lines in white, small but legible on a phone. | Upbeat neutral track from the Instagram library. No voice. |

**Alternate hooks**
1. Header: "What people hear when I say I own a content agency"
2. Header: "Tell me you're self-employed at 23 without telling me"
3. Header: "Who asks what I do for work, ranked by how the conversation goes"

**Alternate endings**
1. Replace the landlord block with: THE BANK / "And your employer is?" / "And your employer's phone number?" / "Sir, that's your phone number."
2. Add a fifth one-line block at the bottom in small text: ME / Also not totally sure what I do.

**Caption:** Which one are you. Be honest, Mom.

**CTA:** The caption question is the only prompt.

### Production
- **Location:** Home desk. · **People:** 1 · **Film:** 10 min · **Edit:** 20 min · **Difficulty:** easy · **Batchable:** yes
- **Props:** Laptop, Headphones or earbuds, Tripod
- **Wardrobe:** Whatever he wears on real client calls.
- **Shot list:**
  1. One static medium shot, 20 seconds of fake-call nodding. Use the best 7 seconds.
- **Editor:** Build the text block in the editor with four colored labels. Check it on a phone: every line must be readable when paused. Keep his face visible in the lower third. Loop cleanly. Do not animate the text in; the point is that it is all there at once.

### Test plan
- **Variants:** Original adaptation; Alternate hook: Header: "What people hear when I say I own a content agency"; Alternate payoff: Bank block replaces the landlord block
- **Brand fit:** 3 of 3 · **Read at:** 24h and 48h · **Primary:** Follows per 1000 non-follower accounts reached
- **Promotion rule:** Brand fit 3. Promote if 48h views reach 3x the trailing median and follows per 1,000 reached are top quartile; an average watch time above the 7-second length confirms the re-read mechanic is working.
- **If it wins, make:** "Types of clients on a first call": four labeled blocks, same shot / "Moving to Florida at 23: who says what" with blocks for locals, his friends back home, his car insurance, and the weather app / "What each platform thinks I do": Instagram, TikTok, LinkedIn, my bank
- **Why this score:** Silent, static and short like his hits, with the strongest small-account evidence in this batch (1,995x), and the header is literally his positioning. All the value is in the writing, so the lines need to be true to his life.
- **Uncertainties:** The mom, friends and landlord lines are written as archetypes. He should swap in the real things those people say; the real version will be funnier and avoids putting words in his mother's mouth.; Dense text can be suppressed if it is unreadable at small sizes; test legibility before posting.; Text-list Reels build less personal connection than acted pieces.

## V003 · Cold call #41: the gym owner closes me instead

**Rank 3 of 50 · Score 85/100 · Day 1 · Batch A: Desk and whiteboard, solo · Business comedy and skits · 18s**

### Reference
- Instagram · @isaaciscalling · https://www.instagram.com/reel/DfrtorhO-iI
- Posted 2026-11-13 · 9.7M views · 142.2x the creator's median · creator had 20,000 followers · checked 2026-11-16 (vidIQ outlier index)
- Footage reviewed: yes · Not available: likes: not returned by the vidIQ index, comments: not returned by the vidIQ index, shares: not returned by the vidIQ index

### Why the reference worked
- **Observed hook:** First spoken sentence (the off-screen caller, screamed): "STOP CALLING ME!" On-screen text at 0:00: "237th dial of the day" / "Open to constructive criticism".
- **First frame:** A young man in glasses, blue dress shirt, tie and an over-ear headset sits in an office chair with his chin on his hand, eyes closed and tired. White wall, drop ceiling and a TV showing a VIZIO logo behind him. A white text box sits over the shot.
- **Structure:** One static take, zero cuts, 16 seconds. 0-3s a calm caption sets an optimistic premise. 4s an off-screen caller explodes. The salesman answers once, calmly, then loses it laughing behind his hand while the caller keeps going. Ends on a text CTA.
- **Mechanism:** Contrast between a polite, deadpan caption and audio that immediately contradicts it. The salesman only gets one line, so the viewer watches his face do the work.
- **Observed evidence:** 9.7M views on a 20K-follower account, 142.2x that creator's 68.5K median. Zero cuts. Text appears before any sound. The salesman speaks one sentence in 16 seconds. No music. The walkthrough does not establish whether the call is real or staged.
- **Hypothesis (not observed):** the caption makes a promise in the first second (this call will go badly) and the payoff arrives by second 4, so almost nobody leaves before it. People who have worked a phone job send it to a coworker with 'this is us'. Comments are other salespeople posting their own worst call.
- **Why it fits Alex:** Same grammar as his biggest results: one shot, a short text line, a face doing the acting. His 13.5M Central Park Reel is a single-shot face reaction, and this adds the one thing his recent feed lacks, which is his actual job (selling a content agency to local businesses).
- **What is borrowed vs new:** Borrowed: single static take, calm caption, a phone voice that derails the call, one calm reply from the seller. New: the prospect does not yell. He is a gym owner who out-sells Alex with Alex's own closing lines, and Alex ends up buying. No profanity, no real call audio, all lines new.

### Alex's version
- **Concept:** Alex cold-calls a gym to pitch content. The owner flips it and runs a hard close on Alex for a gym membership, using the exact pressure lines a salesperson would use. Alex, beaten at his own game, asks if they take Visa.
- **Audience:** People who sell for a living (agency owners, SDRs, realtors, gym staff) and anyone who has been upsold by a gym. · **Emotion:** Amused recognition, with second-hand embarrassment for Alex.
- **First frame:** Alex at a desk, phone to his ear, coffee in the other hand, eyebrows up and confident. Text box top-center already on screen: "Cold call #41 today" and under it "Feeling good about this one".
- **First spoken line:** Hey, is this the owner? It's Alex, I help gyms get a million views on

| Time | Speaker | Dialogue | Action and acting | Camera | On-screen text | Edit | Audio |
|---|---|---|---|---|---|---|---|
| 0-3s | None |  | Alex holds the phone to his ear, sips coffee, gives the camera one small confident nod. Relaxed shoulders. | Static, chest-up, phone on a tripod at eye level, slightly off-center so the phone side of his face is toward camera. | Cold call #41 today / Feeling good about this one | No cuts for the whole video. Text box fades out at 3s. | Two phone ring tones, then a pickup click at 2.8s. No music. |
| 3-6s | Alex | Hey, is this the owner? It's Alex, I help gyms get a million views on | Big salesman smile, leaning forward. He gets cut off mid-sentence and his mouth stays open on the word 'on'. | Same shot. | Word-by-word captions, bottom third, for every spoken line from here. | None. | Room tone. |
| 6-9s | Gym owner (Alex's voice, recorded separately, phone filter) | Alex! Perfect timing. Quick question. Do you currently have a gym membership? | Alex's smile freezes. Eyes flick left, then right. He was not ready to be asked a question. | Same shot. | Captions in a different color (yellow) for the owner. | Drop the owner's line in from a separate recording. | Owner voice with a telephone EQ filter, warm and very upbeat. |
| 9-11s | Alex | I, no, I just moved here, but that's not really why I'm | Flustered, free hand comes up in a small 'wait' gesture. Speeds up as he talks. | Same shot. | Captions. | None. | Room tone. |
| 11-15s | Gym owner | So no plan. Got it. Founder's rate ends today. Alex, do you want results, or do you want excuses? | On 'results or excuses' Alex slowly turns his eyes to the lens and holds for one beat: that is his line and he knows it. Jaw tight. | Same shot. A slow 5% push-in added in the edit from 11s to 15s. | Captions. | Digital push-in only, no cut. | Owner voice, same filter, now in full closer mode. |
| 15-17s | Alex | Do you take Visa? | Long exhale through the nose. He sets the coffee down, pulls a wallet from his pocket and opens it. Says the line quietly, eyes down, completely defeated. | Same shot. | Captions. | Hold the silence for a full second before the line. | Room tone. Wallet leather sound. |
| 17-18s | None |  | Alex looks at the card in his hand, then at the camera, expressionless. | Same shot. | Calls closed: 0 / Times I got closed: 1 | Text box pops on with no animation. Cut to black on the last frame. | One soft cash-register 'ding'. |

**Alternate hooks**
1. Text: "Me, a sales expert, making one quick cold call"
2. Text: "I teach brands how to sell. Watch this."
3. Open cold on the owner's voice at 0s ("Do you currently have a gym membership?") over Alex's frozen face, with the text "I called HIM"

**Alternate endings**
1. Alex hangs up, stares ahead for two seconds, then picks the phone back up and dials again. Text: "Cold call #42. Feeling good about this one." (loops into the opening)
2. Owner's voice after the Visa line: "And would you like to add a personal trainer?" Alex, immediately: "Yes."

**Caption:** He was better at my job than me. #sales

**CTA:** None on screen. Pin a comment: "Call #42 tomorrow."

### Production
- **Location:** Home desk or any plain desk with a wall behind it · **People:** 1 · **Film:** 15 min · **Edit:** 20 min · **Difficulty:** easy · **Batchable:** yes
- **Props:** Phone, Coffee mug, Wallet with a card
- **Wardrobe:** Button-up shirt, sleeves rolled once. Looks like someone trying to seem professional.
- **Shot list:**
  1. One static chest-up take, 18 to 20 seconds, played against the pre-recorded owner lines through an earbud so reactions are timed
  2. Separate audio-only recording of the three owner lines in a brighter, older voice
- **Editor:** Record the owner lines first and play them in one earbud while filming so Alex's pauses are real. Apply a telephone EQ (cut below 300 Hz and above 3.4 kHz) to the owner. Owner captions yellow, Alex captions white. Do not add music. Keep the full second of silence before 'Do you take Visa?'.

### Test plan
- **Variants:** Original adaptation; Alternate hook: "I teach brands how to sell. Watch this."; Alternate payoff: loop back to cold call #42
- **Brand fit:** 3 of 3 · **Read at:** 24h and 48h · **Primary:** Follows per 1000 non-follower accounts reached
- **Promotion rule:** Brand fit 3 (shows him selling the agency). Promote at 48h if views are at least 3x the trailing median and either follows per 1,000 reached or shares per 1,000 reached is top quartile; if follows per 1,000 is 2x median at any view count, re-film the first 3 seconds with the alternate hooks.
- **If it wins, make:** Cold call #42: a restaurant owner puts Alex on hold and the hold music is the owner singing / Cold call #43: the receptionist screens him with questions he cannot answer about his own company / Cold call #44: the owner says yes immediately and Alex does not know what to do next
- **Why this score:** One take, one location, a clear reversal, and the punchline is four words. The numbered cold-call frame makes it a series. It shows his job without explaining it.
- **Uncertainties:** The reference's energy came from an explosive caller; this version trades shock for a status reversal, which is quieter; Alex voicing the owner must sound like a different person or the joke reads as him talking to himself; Whether the reference call was real is unknown, so part of its pull may have been authenticity this staged version does not have

## V004 · First call with the client who said "we're super easy to work with"

**Rank 6 of 50 · Score 84/100 · Day 1 · Batch A: Desk and whiteboard, solo · Business comedy and skits · 11s**

### Reference
- Instagram · @codedbylee · https://www.instagram.com/reel/DfZ9WdEM-fA
- Posted 2026-11-06 · 4.4M views · 1409x the creator's median · creator had 347 followers · checked 2026-11-16 (vidIQ outlier index)
- Footage reviewed: yes · Not available: likes: not returned by the vidIQ index, comments: not returned by the vidIQ index, shares: not returned by the vidIQ index

### Why the reference worked
- **Observed hook:** First audio (a voiceover clip, cutting in mid-thought): "Touch base on February one by time." On-screen text for the whole video: "When u finally land that big client and u get on ur first call and realise why they're a big client..."
- **First frame:** A young man in a white long-sleeve polo and black over-ear headphones sits at a dark desk in front of an open laptop, left hand against his chin, with one centered block of text over the shot. White wall and a wooden door behind him.
- **Structure:** One static take, 0 cuts, about 10 seconds. The caption gives the situation. A dense stream of corporate jargon plays as if from the call. He listens, taps his chin, nods, and answers with one word, "Right."
- **Mechanism:** The caption supplies the context and the audio supplies the overwhelm, so the performer only has to under-react. The one-word reply is the punchline because it is what everyone does on a call they are not following.
- **Observed evidence:** 4.4M views on an account with 347 followers, 1,409x its 3.1K median. No cuts, no music, a single caption. The walkthrough identifies the audio as a pre-existing viral sound that the creator did not write.
- **Hypothesis (not observed):** at under 10 seconds with a long caption, people watch twice (once to read, once to listen), which lifts watch time above 100%. Service providers send it to the one colleague who sits on those calls with them.
- **Why it fits Alex:** Sub-10-second, one shot, caption-led and face-driven is exactly the shape of his 13.5M and 3.6M Reels. This puts that shape on an agency situation.
- **What is borrowed vs new:** Borrowed: static shot, one caption, performer reacting to call audio with a tiny reply. New: the audio is written and recorded for this video (an approval chain that keeps growing), the caption is new, and the reaction is a muted scream followed by two cheerful words.

### Alex's version
- **Concept:** The client said they were easy to work with. On the first call they list who needs to approve every video. The list never ends. Alex mutes himself, silently loses it, unmutes and says "Love that."
- **Audience:** Freelancers, agency people, anyone who has waited on client approvals. · **Emotion:** Recognition, the laugh of 'I have been on this exact call'.
- **First frame:** Alex at his desk in over-ear headphones, laptop open, polite listening face, pen in hand. Centered caption already on screen.
- **First spoken line:** Client voice: "So approvals are simple. It goes to me"

| Time | Speaker | Dialogue | Action and acting | Camera | On-screen text | Edit | Audio |
|---|---|---|---|---|---|---|---|
| 0-3s | Client (Alex's voice, recorded separately, laptop-speaker filter) | So approvals are simple. It goes to me, then Deb, | Alex nods along, writes 'me' and 'Deb' on a sticky note. Pleasant face. | Static, chest-up, laptop edge visible bottom of frame. | First call with the client who said "we're super easy to work with" | Caption stays for the entire video. No captions for speech except the final line. | Client voice through a thin laptop-speaker EQ. No music. |
| 3-7s | Client | then Deb's husband, he's not with the company but he has a great eye, then legal, then we all sleep on it, | Alex stops writing at 'Deb's husband'. The pen hovers. His smile stays on but his eyes go dead. He slowly puts the pen down. | Same. | Same caption. | None. | Client voice continues without a breath. |
| 7-9s | Client | and then my daughter, she's fourteen, she gets TikTok, | Alex reaches forward and taps one key (mute). He turns his head away from the laptop and does a full silent scream at the wall, fists clenched, two seconds, then snaps back to the polite face and taps the key again. | Same. | Same caption. Small red 'MUTED' tag appears top-left while he is muted. | 'MUTED' tag on at 7.2s, off at 8.8s. | Client voice ducks to 30% while muted, returns to full when he unmutes. |
| 9-11s | Alex | Love that. | Bright, warm, thumbs up to the laptop. Completely sincere voice. Hold the smile until the cut. | Same. | Love that. | Cut on the held smile so it loops back into the listening face. | Client starts the next sentence under the cut: "And then if Deb" |

**Alternate hooks**
1. Caption: "Client: we move fast here." then smaller underneath: "The approval process:"
2. Caption: "I asked one question: who signs off on the videos?"
3. Caption: "Day 1 with a new client. I'm 23. I was not ready."

1. Instead of 'Love that', Alex unmutes and asks sweetly, "And does Deb's husband have an email?"
2. He does not unmute. He stays muted, smiling and nodding, while the client keeps listing names and the caption changes to "It has been 40 minutes."

**Caption:** Deb's husband had notes. #agencylife

**CTA:** None.

### Production
- **Location:** Home desk · **People:** 1 · **Film:** 10 min · **Edit:** 15 min · **Difficulty:** easy · **Batchable:** yes
- **Props:** Laptop, Over-ear headphones, Sticky notes and pen
- **Wardrobe:** Clean collared shirt, first-call-with-a-client presentable
- **Shot list:**
  1. One static 11-second take reacting to the pre-recorded client audio played out loud
  2. Audio-only recording of the client lines in one breath, friendly and fast
- **Editor:** Record the client run-on first as one unbroken sentence. Thin it with a laptop-speaker EQ. The silent scream must be fully silent: mute Alex's mic track for those two seconds and let the ducked client voice carry. End the clip one frame after 'Love that' with the client's next words starting, so the loop feels like the call never ends.

### Test plan

All of them are getting posted. | Picks up a stapled stack of printed pages from the tray and fans it at the lens so the numbered list is visible, then drops it on the floor with a thud. | Same shot; he brings the pages within a foot of the lens for one second. | Header stays. | No cut. | Paper flutter, thud. |
| 27-32s | Alex | Day one. Follow so the number moves. I'll update the board tomorrow. | In the bottom corner of the board he writes 'DAY 1: 42,700' and boxes it. Caps the marker, points it at the lens, walks off. | Same shot. Board stays in frame after he exits. | Header stays. | Hold half a second on the empty board with the boxed tally, then cut. | Marker cap click. |

**Alternate hooks**
1. "I need twelve hundred and seventy-three of you a day. Let me show you the math."
2. "My name's Alex, I'm twenty-three, and I just made a promise I might not be able to keep."
3. Silent first two seconds: he writes '42,700 → 100,000' and underlines 'DEC 31', then speaks.

**Alternate endings**
1. He boxes 'DAY 1: 42,700', looks at '1,273 / day' one more time, and quietly writes a small question mark next to it. Walks off.
2. "If I miss it, I'll post that too. Same board." He underlines DEC 31 and leaves.

**Caption:** Day 1. 42,700 → 100,000 by Dec 31. The board gets updated daily.

**CTA:** "Follow so the number moves. I'll update the board tomorrow."

### Production
- **Location:** Home office with the whiteboard from the '2 Types of Clients' Reel · **People:** 1 · **Film:** 20 min · **Edit:** 15 min · **Difficulty:** easy · **Batchable:** yes
- **Props:** Whiteboard, Two dry-erase markers, Printed, stapled, numbered list of the 50 video titles
- **Wardrobe:** Black tee, the same look as the whiteboard client skit
- **Shot list:**
  1. One continuous take, 32 s, starting with '42,700' pre-written. Shoot 4 takes, erasing the board between each.
  2. Photograph the finished board for the story and for tomorrow's Day 2 open.
- **Editor:** No cuts. Pin the header for the full duration. Subtitles lower third. No music. Before filming, recompute the board with the real numbers for posting day: current followers, days left to Dec 31, and the per-day figure. The script uses the Nov 16 values (42,700, 45 days, 1,273).

### Test plan
- **Variants:** Original adaptation; Alternate hook: "I need twelve hundred and seventy-three of you a day. Let me show you the math."; Alternate payoff: the small question mark next to 1,273 / day
- **Brand fit:** 3 of 3 · **Read at:** 24h and 48h · **Primary:** Follows per 1000 non-follower accounts reached
- **Promotion rule:** Brand fit 3: promote Day 1 to the main audience if follows per 1,000 reached are at or above the trailing median, because the series needs existing followers to see the bet; continue the daily series only if Days 1 to 3 together beat median follows per 1,000.
- **If it wins, make:** Day 2: same board, new tally, plus the single best and worst video from yesterday in 20 seconds / Day 7: he recomputes the per-day number live and reacts to whether it went up or down / Day 15: halfway check where the audience's most-liked comment picks one video he has to make
- **Why this score:** Gives strangers an explicit reason to follow (a dated, checkable bet), reuses his own proven whiteboard prop, and creates a daily series with a built-in end card. The 6.0M reference came from an account with a physical product and a famous goal; a follower-count goal is less visual.
- **Uncertainties:** A follower goal is less tangible than a product demo; the whiteboard math has to carry the middle.; The numbers on the board must be recomputed for the real posting day.; Committing to a daily series in public raises the cost of missing a day.; 'I wrote fifty videos this week' is true only once this 50-script package is in hand; keep the line only if all 50 will be posted.

## V009 · I never say a word: selling "90 days" to a guy who thinks it's too long

**Rank 12 of 50 · Score 82/100 · Day 2 · Batch A: Desk and whiteboard, solo · Business comedy and skits · 27s**

### Reference
- Instagram · @ejthemaker · https://www.instagram.com/reel/Ddhxc7CyfrM
- Posted 2026-09-20 · 3.7M views · 22.4x the creator's median · creator had 811,000 followers · checked 2026-11-16 (vidIQ outlier index)
- Footage reviewed: yes · Not available: likes: not returned by the vidIQ index, comments: not returned by the vidIQ index, shares: not returned by the vidIQ index

### Why the reference worked
- **Observed hook:** "Oh, that's a really nice laptop. How much is it?" On-screen text for the whole video: "How easily manipulated we are".
- **First frame:** A young man in a black coat with a faux fur collar stands by a counter holding a spiral notebook with red writing, "$1,000" faintly visible through the page.
- **Structure:** 35.7 seconds, 14 cuts, one actor in two roles. The customer asks the price. The seller never speaks: he shows $1,000 (too much), then $800 (still too much), then writes $1,000 crossed out next to $800. The customer is thrilled, buys, and walks away congratulating himself.
- **Mechanism:** A real sales principle (price anchoring) shown instead of explained. The seller's silence makes the viewer read the notepad, and the customer's self-congratulation is the punchline because the viewer saw the trick.
- **Observed evidence:** 3.7M views, 22.4x the creator's 164.4K median, on an 811K-follower account. No music, only dialogue, marker and paper sounds. One persistent line of text. The seller has zero lines.
- **Hypothesis (not observed):** viewers feel smart for spotting the trick and share it with a 'this is literally you' message. Being a large account, part of the view count is the creator's existing audience, so the format's own pull is less certain than the small-account references.
- **Why it fits Alex:** Alex's 2.8M skit was a whiteboard and a marker. A silent seller writing on that same whiteboard reuses a prop his audience already rewarded, and the punchline is written, so it works muted.
- **What is borrowed vs new:** Borrowed: silent seller, written offers, a buyer who talks himself into the same deal. New: nothing is discounted and no price appears. The offer is his real one (1,000,000 views in 90 days) and the only change is rewording '90 days' as '3 months'. Whiteboard instead of notepad, all dialogue new.

### Alex's version
- **Concept:** A prospect loves '1,000,000 views' but says 90 days is too long. Alex, who never speaks, crosses out '90 days' and writes '3 months'. The prospect is delighted and leaves bragging that he negotiated.
- **Audience:** Sales and marketing people, business owners, anyone who enjoys watching a negotiation go wrong for the confident guy. · **Emotion:** Feeling smarter than the prospect.
- **First frame:** Alex beside the whiteboard, marker in hand, calm closed-mouth smile. The board reads in large letters: "1,000,000 VIEWS". Persistent text top of frame: "I never said a word".
- **First spoken line:** Prospect: "A million views? Okay, I'm listening. How long's that take?"

How long's that take? | Chewing gum, arms crossed, impressed but trying not to show it. | Prospect: chest-up, angled toward camera-left. Seller shot: waist-up beside the whiteboard, angled toward camera-right. | I never said a word | Open on a 1-second shot of Alex and the board, then cut to the prospect. Persistent top text all video. Captions for the prospect only. | Voice, no music. |

2. Prospect turns back at the door: "Could you do twelve weeks?" Alex uncaps the marker without changing his face.

**Caption:** Same deal. He's thrilled.

**CTA:** None.

### Production
- **Location:** Room with the whiteboard used in '2 Types of Clients', plus a hallway or doorway for the walk-away · **People:** 1 · **Film:** 30 min · **Edit:** 30 min · **Difficulty:** medium · **Batchable:** yes
- **Props:** Whiteboard, Black dry-erase marker, Sunglasses, Chewing gum

  5. Prospect: three lines, chest-up
  6. Prospect: walk-away from behind with the last line
- **Editor:** Write the seller's board work in large block letters that fill the frame so it is readable on a phone. Keep '1,000,000 VIEWS' on the board in every seller shot. Never give the seller a caption or a sound other than the marker. No music. The final line should be recorded on location while walking so it trails off naturally.

### Test plan
- **Variants:** Original adaptation; Alternate hook: persistent text 'He thinks he won'; Alternate payoff: the 'twelve weeks' callback
- **Brand fit:** 3 of 3 · **Read at:** 24h and 48h · **Primary:** Follows per 1000 non-follower accounts reached
- **Promotion rule:** Brand fit 3 (his actual offer is on the board the whole time). Promote at 48h if views are at least 3x the trailing median and follows per 1,000 reached are top quartile; also log profile visits, since the board states the offer.
- **If it wins, make:** Silent seller: prospect says 'I want to post every day' and rejects '7 videos a week' but accepts 'one a day' / Silent seller: prospect refuses 'film for 2 hours' and loves 'film for 120 minutes' / Silent seller meets a second silent buyer who brings his own whiteboard
- **Why this score:** It reuses the exact prop from his proven skit, puts his real offer on screen for 27 seconds without a pitch, and the joke is written on the board, so it survives sound-off viewing.
- **Uncertainties:** The reference is from an 811K-follower account with a 22.4x multiple, so evidence that the format alone drives views is moderate; The joke depends on viewers doing the 90 days equals 3 months math instantly; the insert close-up is there to help; Stating '1,000,000 views in 90 days' on screen is his real offer; keep it as a goal he sells, not a guaranteed outcome claim in the caption

## V010 · "Can we get it sooner?": the edit gets worse every time the deadline moves

**Rank 17 of 50 · Score 81/100 · Day 2 · Batch A: Desk and whiteboard, solo · Business comedy and skits · 36s**

### Reference
- TikTok · @coleevanderproductions · https://www.tiktok.com/@coleevanderproductions/video/7693588144616181022
- Posted 2026-10-06 · 2.2M views · 204.2x the creator's median · creator had 16,400 followers · checked 2026-11-16 (vidIQ outlier index)
- Footage reviewed: yes · Not available: likes: not returned by the vidIQ index, comments: not returned by the vidIQ index, shares: not returned by the vidIQ index, same video also indexed on Instagram at 1.9M views (https://www.instagram.com/reel/DeKbI2IA1qD); that upload was not watched separately

### Why the reference worked
- **Observed hook:** "Hey, quick update before we start filming." (off-screen client). Static header text: "POV: When a client changes their budget last minute".
- **First frame:** A man stands in a hotel parking lot smiling into the camera while holding a heavy dual-handle gimbal camera rig with monitor and mic, under a white text box.
- **Structure:** 49 seconds, 4 jump cuts. An off-screen client lowers the budget in steps ($1,200, $750, $300, $75). After each step a jump cut shows the videographer holding worse equipment (full rig, bare camera, laptop webcam, old iPhone). He stays cheerful and literal. Ends with him sighing 'All right, let's shoot' and walking off.
- **Mechanism:** An escalation ladder where the visual is the punchline. Each cut answers the client's ask with an object, so the joke lands even without the dialogue, and the viewer stays to see how low it goes.
- **Observed evidence:** 2.2M views on TikTok (204.2x the creator's 10.8K median, 16.4K followers) and 1.9M on Instagram. One performer, one location, four prop swaps. The client is never seen. Low music bed under clean dialogue.
- **Hypothesis (not observed):** service providers tag the client-facing person on their team, and each downgrade resets attention so retention holds through 49 seconds. The pattern is predictable by step two, which lets viewers anticipate and enjoy step four.
- **Why it fits Alex:** It is a one-man client skit like his 2.8M '2 Types of Clients', and the downgrade gag is visual enough to work muted, which matches how his biggest Reels play.
- **What is borrowed vs new:** Borrowed: off-screen client, a stepped demand, a jump cut to a visibly worse version after each step. New: the variable is the deadline, not the budget. What degrades is the finished video itself, ending with Alex performing the ad live through a cardboard phone frame. All dialogue is new.

### Alex's version
- **Concept:** A client keeps moving the deadline up. Each time, Alex cheerfully shows what that timeline buys: a full edit, a rough phone cut, raw footage, and finally Alex himself performing the gym ad live inside a cardboard phone frame.
- **Audience:** Editors, videographers, agency owners, and anyone who has been asked for something 'by end of day'. · **Emotion:** Amused vindication.
- **First frame:** Alex at a desk, proudly turning a laptop toward the camera. On its screen a polished vertical video with captions is paused. Header text: "POV: the client moves the deadline".
- **First spoken line:** Client (off-screen): "Hey, love the plan. Any chance we get it Friday instead of next week?"

| Time | Speaker | Dialogue | Action and acting | Camera | On-screen text | Edit | Audio |
|---|---|---|---|---|---|---|---|
| 0-4s | Client (off-screen, Alex's voice pitched slightly down) | Hey, love the plan. Any chance we get it Friday instead of next week? | Alex nods like a good sport, still presenting the laptop with the polished edit on screen. | Static, waist-up, desk in front of him. Client voice comes from camera-left, so Alex looks just left of lens. | POV: the client moves the deadline | Header stays all video. Word-by-word captions. Small label bottom-left: "NEXT WEEK". | Voice only, quiet upbeat bed at 8%. |

2. Client: "Actually, the launch got pushed. Next week is fine." Alex sets the cardboard down very gently and walks out of frame without a word.

**Caption:** You can have it fast or you can have it. #clientsbelike

**CTA:** None.

### Production
- **Location:** Home desk or office desk · **People:** 1 · **Film:** 30 min · **Edit:** 35 min · **Difficulty:** medium · **Batchable:** yes
- **Props:** Laptop with a finished vertical video on screen, Phone with a draft version and a raw clip saved, Cardboard cut into a phone-shaped frame, about 18 by 30 inches, with a hole for his face
- **Wardrobe:** Same outfit in every shot so the jump cuts read as one conversation
- **Shot list:**
  1. Shot 1: laptop with polished edit (0 to 7s)
  2. Shot 2: phone with draft cut (7 to 17s)
  3. Shot 3: phone with raw footage (17 to 26s)
  4. Shot 4: cardboard frame live performance, music question, hum (26 to 36s)
  5. Audio-only: all client lines
- **B-roll:** A real finished vertical video from his own library for the laptop screen; A deliberately bad 5-second raw clip of a gym floor or any location, thumb in frame
- **Editor:** Lock the tripod and do not move it between shots so each jump cut only changes what Alex is holding. Change the corner label on each cut (NEXT WEEK, FRIDAY, TOMORROW, RIGHT NOW). Pitch the client voice down two semitones and pan it slightly left. Kill the music bed at 22s and keep it off to the end.

### Test plan
- **Variants:** Original adaptation; Alternate hook: cold open on the cardboard performance, then '3 deadline changes earlier'; Alternate payoff: client asks for the file and Alex hands over the cardboard
- **Brand fit:** 3 of 3 · **Read at:** 24h and 48h · **Primary:** Follows per 1000 non-follower accounts reached
- **Promotion rule:** Brand fit 3. Promote at 48h if views are at least 3x the trailing median and either follows or shares per 1,000 reached are top quartile; if 3-second retention is weak but completion is strong, re-post with the cold-open hook.
- **If it wins, make:** Same ladder with the budget: full shoot, one take, a photo, a text description of what the video would have been / Same ladder with 'can you make it shorter': 60s, 15s, 3s, a single frame / Same ladder with the number of people who need to appear on camera, ending with Alex playing the whole staff
- **Why this score:** Strongest physical payoff in this batch. The cardboard-frame performance uses his acting and reads with no sound, and the ladder structure is proven on two platforms by the reference.
- **Uncertainties:** 36 seconds is longer than his silent hits; the first cut must arrive by second 7; Needs three pieces of real footage prepared in advance; The humming button is delivery-dependent

## V011 · "Can we hop on video real quick?" (one-take scramble)

**Rank 22 of 50 · Score 79/100 · Day 2 · Batch A: Desk and whiteboard, solo · Business comedy and skits · 30s**

### Reference
- Instagram · @jayzee_here · https://www.instagram.com/reel/DdT5j6tIOQC
- Posted 2026-09-15 · 19.8M views · 1558.8x the creator's median · creator had 8,000 followers · checked 2026-11-16 (vidIQ outlier index)
- Footage reviewed: yes · Not available: likes (not returned by the index), comments (not returned by the index), shares (not returned by the index), metrics_source_url (view count comes from the vidIQ index, not a public page)

### Why the reference worked
- **Observed hook:** "Good afternoon, everybody." (meeting-host voiceover at 0:01). On-screen text for the full video: "POV: Working from home and you’re not notified that it’s an open camera meeting."
- **First frame:** A woman in a white tank top, shorts and large headphones sits at a desk with a laptop and reaches for her coffee mug.
- **Structure:** One persistent POV caption. A pre-recorded host voice announces a cameras-on meeting and starts naming attendees, which acts as a countdown. In a single uncut take she physically transforms from loungewear to composed professional and lands a sweet greeting as her name is called.
- **Mechanism:** An external voice sets a deadline the performer cannot control, and the viewer watches a real-time race in one take. The lack of cuts proves the scramble is real, and the calm greeting at the end is the release.
- **Observed evidence:** 19.8M views on an 8K-follower account with a 12.7K median (1558.8x). 41 seconds, 0 cuts, no CTA, does not loop. The host voiceover runs from 0:01 and names attendees from 0:12.
- **Hypothesis (not observed):** retention holds because the name roll-call is a visible clock, so viewers stay to see if she makes it. Remote workers send it to coworkers with 'us on every Monday call'. The one-take format invites comments about how she pulled it off.
- **Why it fits Alex:** It is a pure physical-acting piece with no dialogue until the last line, which uses his acting background the way '2 Types of Clients' (2.8M, his highest comment rate) did. The client on the other end ties it to his agency.
- **What is borrowed vs new:** Borrowed: persistent POV caption, an off-screen voice as a countdown, a one-take physical scramble. New: the voice is a client and his team, the setting is a Florida apartment at 2 pm, the gags are his (blazer over swim trunks, cereal, a stack of business books as a backdrop), and the ending adds a second reveal instead of stopping at the greeting.

### Alex's version
- **Concept:** A client calls unannounced and asks to switch to video with the whole team watching. Alex, who works from home in Florida and looks like it, has 20 seconds to become a CEO.
- **Audience:** Remote workers, freelancers and agency people 18-34. · **Emotion:** Suspense turning into laughter
- **First frame:** Alex at his desk in a tank top and swim trunks, hair a mess, eating cereal from the box, laptop open. Caption on screen.
- **First spoken line:** Client (voiceover): "Alex! Hey, quick thing. Mind if we flip to video?"

| Time | Speaker | Dialogue | Action and acting | Camera | On-screen text | Edit | Audio |
|---|---|---|---|---|---|---|---|
| 0-3s | Client (pre-recorded voiceover, older, cheerful) | Alex! Hey, quick thing. Mind if we flip to video? | Alex freezes mid-chew, handful of cereal at his mouth, eyes slide to the laptop. | Wide static shot on tripod showing desk, chair, floor around it and a bare wall behind. Whole body visible. | POV: you work from home and the client says "video real quick" | One take, no cuts. Caption stays for the whole video. | Laptop-speaker voice filter on the client. No music. |
| 3-7s | Client (voiceover) | I've got the whole team in here. Say hi, everybody. | A beat of pure dread, then he explodes into motion: sets the cereal box on the floor out of frame, wipes his mouth with his forearm. | Same shot. | Same caption. | No cut. | Under the client line, 4 or 5 muffled voices say 'hey Alex' (record yourself 4 times and layer). |
| 7-13s | Client (voiceover) | We've got Dana from finance, Mark, the new intern... one sec, the camera's being weird on our end. | He grabs a blazer off the chair back and throws it on over the tank top, buttons the wrong button, fixes it. We can't see you yet. | He sprints off frame left, returns with a stack of thick business books and a small plant, builds a 'backdrop' on the shelf behind his chair in two seconds, kicks a pile of laundry out of frame. | Same shot. | Same caption. | No cut. | One book thuds. |
| 19-24s | Alex | Hey team! So good to see everybody. | He drops into the chair, one breath, face switches instantly to a warm, relaxed, senior-partner smile. Clicks the trackpad. Small wave. | Same shot. | Same caption. | No cut. | Video-call join chime as he clicks. |
| 24-30s | Client (voiceover) then Alex | Client: Love the office. Hey, can you grab that contract off the printer?  Alex: ...Yep. Absolutely. | Smile stays, eyes die. He stands slowly. The blazer ends at his waist and the bright swim trunks and bare feet are fully visible. He side-shuffles out of frame, keeping his top half facing the laptop. | Same shot. Make sure trunks are in frame when he stands. | Same caption. | Cut to black the moment he exits frame. | Silence after 'Absolutely', then one flip-flop slap sound as he exits. |

**Alternate hooks**
1. Caption: "POV: 2 pm in Florida and the client wants cameras on"
2. Open on the laptop chime and Alex's face only, cereal mid-air, then pull the tripod back to the wide as he starts moving.
3. Caption: "The 20 seconds before every 'professional' video call"

**Alternate endings**
1. He nails the greeting, then the client says: "Oh, we're still on audio only, the camera never worked." Alex slowly unbuttons the blazer and picks the cereal back up.
2. He sits, smiles, says hello, and the plant falls off the shelf behind him. He does not react. Hold three seconds.

**Caption:** I am a professional from the waist up. #wfh

**CTA:** None.

### Production
- **Location:** Home desk with a bare wall and a shelf behind the chair · **People:** 1 · **Film:** 35 min · **Edit:** 20 min · **Difficulty:** medium · **Batchable:** yes
- **Props:** Laptop, Cereal box, Blazer on chair back, Glass of water, 4 thick business books, Small plant, Laundry pile, Swim trunks
- **Wardrobe:** Tank top and bright swim trunks, barefoot, then blazer on top
- **Shot list:**
  1. Record the client voiceover track first (pitch your voice down, older, cheerful) with exact timings: 0-3, 3-7, 7-13, 13-19, 24-27.
  2. Play the track out loud from a second device while filming so the movement hits each line.
  3. One wide locked-off take, full body in frame. Plan on 6 to 8 takes.
- **Editor:** Zero cuts in the body. Lay the clean voiceover over the take and apply a laptop-speaker EQ. Add the persistent caption at top. Add join chime at 0:22 and one flip-flop slap at the exit. Hard cut to black on exit. If the take runs over 32 s, speed the 7-19 s section to 110%.

### Test plan

### Why the reference worked
- **Observed hook:** "You could rebrand anything to sound healthy, but how easy would it be to do the opposite and rebrand blueberries to sound unhealthy?"
- **First frame:** A man in glasses and a black t-shirt at a white desk with a clear clamshell of fresh blueberries in front of him, studio wall and neon sign behind.
- **Structure:** A reverse-premise question, 'Let's find out', then a step-by-step build where each choice (container, name, mascot, tagline, background) is explained in one line while the product mockup changes on the desk, then the final reveal and a 'what should I rebrand next' prompt.
- **Mechanism:** The viewer watches a product change in stages and wants to see the finished object. Each step is a small design decision with a stated reason, so the entertainment doubles as a lesson without a lecture.
- **Observed evidence:** 4.6M views, 62.4x the creator's 73.8K median, 100s, 512.5K followers. The mockup evolves on the desk through the video. Series prompt in the close. The original relies on custom illustration and motion graphics.
- **Hypothesis (not observed):** the before/after object is the retention device, and asking what to rebrand next generates comments that double as a content queue. Viewers send it to a friend with the invented product name.
- **Why it fits Alex:** It demonstrates what his agency sells (framing changes perceived value) on a three-dollar object, with a result anyone can judge on sight. His time in France gives him a real reason for the French name, and Canva keeps it inside his skills.
- **What is borrowed vs new:** Borrowed: 'can I rebrand X to sound like the opposite', staged build on a desk, series question at the end. New: the product, the direction (cheap to luxury, played for comedy), every name and line, no mascot or illustration, and a cut to about 45 seconds.

## V015 · "Say it slower." Three business owners hear themselves

**Rank 5 of 50 · Score 85/100 · Day 3 · Batch B: Desk phone skits with wardrobe changes · Business comedy and skits · 38s**

### Reference
- YouTube Shorts · Jamie Brindle · https://www.youtube.com/shorts/-1D9KJsMZng
- Posted 2026-10-15 · 2.0M views · 844x the creator's median · creator had 41,600 followers · checked 2026-11-16 (vidIQ outlier index)
- Footage reviewed: yes · Not available: likes: not returned by the vidIQ index, comments: not returned by the vidIQ index, shares: not returned by the vidIQ index, creator_median_views: the YouTube outlier tool returns only a breakout multiple (844x), creator_followers is YouTube subscribers (41.6K)

### Why the reference worked
- **Observed hook:** "We thought we needed you, but we're just going to use AI for this."
- **First frame:** Medium close-up of a bearded man in round glasses and a grey zip-up jacket, looking slightly off camera and gesturing, labelled "Client".
- **Structure:** 60 seconds, 14 cuts, three mini-scenes. In each, the same actor plays a labelled Client (new outfit per scene) and a labelled Freelancer. The client opens with a common unfair ask and the freelancer answers calmly in a way that flips the status. In the third scene the freelancer's reply becomes a repeated line ("Ask me again") and the video ends on its second use.
- **Mechanism:** Wish fulfilment for freelancers: the reply they wish they had given. The on-screen role labels make every cut instantly readable, and the repeated closing line gives the last scene a rhythm the viewer can predict.
- **Observed evidence:** 2M views, 844x breakout, 41.6K subscribers. Title is a share instruction: "Send to a freelancer who's working too hard". No music. Word-by-word captions. Outfit changes separate the scenes.
- **Hypothesis (not observed):** the title tells viewers who to send it to, and freelancers forward it as a stand-in for what they cannot say to their own client. The repeated line is quotable, so it shows up in comments.
- **Why it fits Alex:** Labelled one-man client dialogue is the grammar of his 2.8M skit, and three short scenes let him play three distinct characters, which is where his acting background shows.
- **What is borrowed vs new:** Borrowed: labels, one actor in two roles, three quick scenes, a repeated button line. New: the seller does not argue or win with policy. He says three words, "Say it slower", and the business owner defeats their own objection by repeating it. Scenarios are about content and marketing, and the third scene breaks the pattern.

### Alex's version
- **Concept:** Three business owners each give Alex a reason they do not need content. He answers every one with the same three words: "Say it slower." Hearing their own sentence at half speed, each owner realises the problem. The third refuses to repeat it, and that is how Alex knows he has him.
- **Audience:** Business owners who are on the fence about content, and the marketers who pitch them. · **Emotion:** Satisfaction, the 'he got him' feeling.
- **First frame:** Alex as Owner 1: quarter-zip pullover, reading glasses pushed up on his head, arms crossed, mid-sentence. Label top-left: "Business owner".

- **Mechanism:** A fast type-list where every entry ends with the same physical punchline. The outfit change signals 'new caller' without a word, and the hang-up lets the viewer enjoy a response they are not allowed to give in real life.
- **Observed evidence:** 1.2M views on a 7.4K-follower account with a 1.8K median (660.8x). Four one-line callers in under 20 seconds. Phone ring and receiver slam sound effects. No music. Caption: "I can't be the only one that deals with this."

- **Why it fits Alex:** It is the '2 Types of Clients' idea (client behaviour, acted, at a desk) compressed to 20 seconds with four characters. The repeated physical action reads without sound.
- **What is borrowed vs new:** Borrowed: same desk, outfit per caller, one line each on a filtered voice, a physical sign-off every time. New: it is one repeated question from Alex after delivering a video, the four answers are new, the action is placing the phone face down, and the last beat changes the action (phone goes in a drawer, the drawer buzzes).

### Alex's version

  3. Pool: drink sip and grin to camera
  4. Desk: asleep on keyboard, side angle
  5. Desk: phone buzzing with 47 notifications close-up
  6. Desk: head lift with key marks, look to camera, start typing
- **B-roll:** 2 s of palm trees and water for a cutaway if a pool take is short
- **Editor:** First 10 s: 50% slow motion, warm grade, bossa nova. Pitch-warp the music down over the last half second and freeze the frame. Hard cut to the desk on a phone buzz, normal speed, cooler grade, no music. Captions: line 1 for 0-10 s, 'Actual footage:' for 10-20 s. Use a mockup for the notification screen so no real client name appears.

### Test plan
- **Variants:** Original adaptation; Alternate hook: "What people think owning an agency at 23 looks like"; Alternate payoff: the pool was his laptop wallpaper
- **Brand fit:** 3 of 3 · **Read at:** 24h and 48h · **Primary:** Follows per 1000 non-follower accounts reached
- **Promotion rule:** Brand fit 3: promote if 48h views are at least 3x the trailing median and shares per 1,000 reached are top quartile, since owners sending it to owners is the expected behaviour.
- **If it wins, make:** "When the client says 'no rush'" vs actual footage / "Taking a real day off" vs actual footage (answering messages in the ocean) / "Hiring your first editor so you can relax" vs actual footage (re-editing everything at 2 am)
- **Why this score:** Silent, text-led and clearly about his business, with a visual contrast that reads in two seconds. The fantasy half risks looking like the lifestyle flex he wants to avoid, which is why it is short and immediately punctured.
- **Uncertainties:** If the pool half runs long it reads as a flex; keep it at 10 s.; Two locations and a day-night split make it a two-session shoot.; The reference used a famous soundtrack as its dream signal; the pitch-warp replacement is untested.

## V028 · Why my 9-second video with no words got 13.5 million plays (breakdown of his own Central Park Reel)

**Rank 19 of 50 · Score 81/100 · Day 4 · Batch E: Green screen and screen breakdowns · Marketing experiments and case studies · 38s**

### Reference
- Instagram · @ameliagonzamonte · https://www.instagram.com/reel/De2nFEHx4Uy
- Posted 2026-10-23 · 7.2M views · 55.4x the creator's median · creator had 272,100 followers · checked 2026-11-16 (vidIQ outlier index)

### Why the reference worked
- **Observed hook:** "This video proves that curiosity gaps sell."
- **First frame:** A presenter holding a small clip-on mic to her mouth, looking into the lens, with an inset screenshot of the video she is about to break down in front of her chest.
- **Structure:** One-sentence claim, then the weak version the creator could have made, then the view count, then 'two elements of psychology' numbered on screen (one for watch, one for follow), a 'did you spot it yet?' beat, and an ending that cuts off mid-sentence toward the caption.
- **Mechanism:** A numbered promise in the first ten seconds (two things, one makes you watch, one makes you follow) with the subject video visible the whole time, so the viewer checks each claim against the picture.
- **Observed evidence:** 7.2M views, 55.4x the creator's 130.8K median, 49.4s. The subject video's on-screen text and its view count are shown. On-screen counters '1 -> watch' and '2 -> follow'. Ending is cut mid-sentence by design.
- **Hypothesis (not observed):** creators and business owners save and send breakdowns that give them two named, reusable moves, and the 'did you spot it' beat makes them rewatch the inset. The cut-off ending pushes people to the caption, which adds watch time.
- **Why it fits Alex:** He owns a better subject than most breakdown creators: a 9-second silent Reel at 13.5M plays. Breaking down his own hit proves expertise with his own receipts and needs no third-party footage. Green screen is the grammar of that same hit.
- **What is borrowed vs new:** Borrowed: claim-first open, numbered reasons, a spot-it beat, an abrupt end. New: the subject is his own video, the reasons are his own reading of it, and the ending is a joke about not explaining jokes instead of a caption push.

### Alex's version
- **Concept:** Alex green-screens himself over his own Central Park Reel and gives three reasons it travelled, each one visible in the frame behind him. He labels them as his read, not proof, and ends by refusing to explain the ending.
- **Audience:** Creators, marketers and business owners who want to know why a specific video worked; anyone who saw the original. · **Emotion:** Curiosity with a payoff of 'oh, that's why'
- **First frame:** Alex small in the lower third, the Central Park-as-parking-lot Reel paused behind him, a view counter graphic reading 13.5M above his head.
- **First spoken line:** This video is nine seconds long, I don't say a single word, and it got thirteen and a half million plays.

**Caption:** My read on why 9 silent seconds beat everything else I've posted. The original is pinned on my page.

- **Brand fit:** 3 of 3 · **Read at:** 24h and 48h · **Primary:** Follows per 1000 non-follower accounts reached
- **Promotion rule:** Brand fit 3: promote at 48h if views are at least 3x his trailing median and either follows per 1,000 reached or shares per 1,000 reached is top quartile; this is a profile-visit driver, so also check profile visits before deciding.

## V029 · "As someone who does this for a living... no notes": deadpan green screen over a brand's slang post

**Rank 20 of 50 · Score 81/100 · Day 5 · Batch E: Green screen and screen breakdowns · Marketing experiments and case studies · 10s**

### Reference
- TikTok · @akuntbergwitz4 · https://www.tiktok.com/@akuntbergwitz4/video/7681479570544971038
- Posted 2026-09-04 · 2.9M views · 33x the creator's median · creator had 1,800 followers · checked 2026-11-16 (vidIQ outlier index)
- Footage reviewed: yes · Not available: likes: not returned by the index, comments: not returned by the index, shares: not returned by the index

### Why the reference worked
- **Observed hook:** "So, it seems like McDonald's has came up with a new marketing strategy, and..."
- **First frame:** A man's face in low light looking into the camera, the text "McDonald's marketing Strategy" centered, and a green-screened screenshot of a brand's own social post in front of his chest.
- **Structure:** One take. He introduces the brand post as a 'strategy', trails off, and under-reacts with a polite non-judgment. He never says what is odd about the image.
- **Mechanism:** The creator withholds the joke. The viewer sees the image, gets it on their own, and the comment section becomes the place where people say it out loud.
- **Observed evidence:** 2.9M views on an account with 1.8K followers, 33.0x its 88K median, 9s, zero cuts, quiet atmospheric track, no CTA. The humour in the reference depends on a suggestive reading of the brand image.
- **Hypothesis (not observed):** a nine-second clip where the punchline is left unsaid gets rewatched to confirm the reading, and viewers tag the brand or a friend. Brand posts are public and constantly renewed, so the format restocks itself.
- **Why it fits Alex:** Green screen plus a deadpan face is exactly his 13.5M Reel, and his best share rate came from a brand reaction (AirPods, 0.92% on TikTok). The audit found his brand-tag jokes fail when the visual is unrelated; here the brand's own post is the visual.
- **What is borrowed vs new:** Borrowed: green screen over a brand's own post, polite under-reaction, joke left unsaid. New: no innuendo; the target is a serious company using stale slang, and the line is built on the fact that this is his profession.

### Alex's version
- **Concept:** Alex stands in front of a screenshot of a serious company's social post that uses slang or a meme format far past its date. He introduces himself as a professional, offers 'no notes', waits, and then admits he has notes. He never says what they are.
- **Audience:** Anyone who has cringed at a brand trying to be relatable; social media managers; his existing meme audience. · **Emotion:** Secondhand embarrassment, enjoyed
- **First frame:** The brand's post fills the top two thirds of the frame with its handle visible. Alex is small at the bottom, eyes already on the lens, expression neutral. Centered text: '[Brand type]'s social media strategy'.
- **First spoken line:** So. As someone who does this for a living.

| Time | Speaker | Dialogue | Action and acting | Camera | On-screen text | Edit | Audio |
|---|---|---|---|---|---|---|---|
| 0-3s | Alex | So. As someone who does this for a living. | Hands clasped in front of him like a consultant about to present. Neutral, respectful face. Does not look at the post. | Green screen. Alex bottom-center, chest up. Screenshot of the brand post behind and above him, handle visible. | a bank's social media strategy (swap 'bank' for the real category) | Text on from frame one. No zooms. | His voice, dry. Very quiet neutral room tone, no music. |
| 3-6s | Alex | No notes. | Small, firm nod. Then he turns his head and actually looks at the post for the first time. Holds the look. His jaw tightens slightly. | Same | (text stays) | None. The pause is the content: hold a full two seconds of silence after the line. | Silence |
| 6-10s | Alex | I have notes. | Turns back to the lens. Same polite face, one notch more strained. A slow blink. He does not elaborate. | Same | (text stays) | Cut half a second after the blink so it loops back into 'So.' | His voice, quieter than before |

**Alternate hooks**
1. I get paid to make content for brands, and I'd like to say one thing about this.
2. Somebody at this company approved this. In a meeting.
3. I'm 23 and even I don't say this anymore.

**Alternate endings**
1. After the long look he turns back and says, 'I would like to meet whoever approved this. To hire them.' Straight face, cut.
2. After the long look he says nothing at all, just slowly raises a notepad and clicks a pen. Cut.

**Caption:** Respectfully. (screenshot from their public page)

**CTA:** None

### Production
- **Location:** Home, any wall; Instagram green screen · **People:** 1 · **Film:** 8 min · **Edit:** 8 min · **Difficulty:** easy · **Batchable:** yes
- **Props:** Phone, One screenshot (see editor instructions), Notepad and pen for alternate ending 2
- **Wardrobe:** A collared shirt or quarter-zip so he looks like the professional in the room
- **Shot list:**
  1. One 10-second green-screen take per screenshot; shoot three different brand screenshots in one sitting
- **Editor:** How to find the screenshot, any week: open the public Instagram, X or LinkedIn page of a company in a serious category (bank, insurer, airline, tax software, mattress brand) and scroll for a post that uses slang or a meme template that peaked at least six months earlier. Screenshot it with the handle and date visible. Do not edit the post. Do not say anything factual about the company: the video is opinion and the joke is his reaction. Skip posts about anything sensitive (layoffs, safety, health). Replace 'bank' in the on-screen text with the real category. Do not add music.

### Test plan
- **Variants:** Original adaptation; Alternate hook: 'Somebody at this company approved this. In a meeting.'; Alternate payoff: 'To hire them.'
- **Brand fit:** 2 of 3 · **Read at:** 24h and 48h · **Primary:** Follows per 1000 non-follower accounts reached
- **Promotion rule:** Brand fit 2: promote at 48h only if views are at least 3x his trailing median and shares per 1,000 reached is top quartile; if a version with the 'for a living' line converts better than one without, keep that line in every sequel.
- **If it wins, make:** Same reaction to a billboard he photographs himself while driving in Florida / Same reaction to a local business's flyer that does it right: 'No notes.' and this time he means it, and says why in the caption / Same reaction to one of his own old posts: 'I have notes.'
- **Why this score:** Closest structural match to his biggest Reel, with the one-line addition of his profession. It can be remade weekly with a new screenshot. The laugh depends on finding a post that is funny on sight and on him holding the silence.
- **Uncertainties:** Quality of the screenshot decides the result; The audit shows his recent brand-tag memes averaged under 2,000 plays; the difference here is that the brand's post is the visual, which is a hypothesis; Brand fit is 2; A brand could respond; keep it to reaction and opinion

- **Structure:** A superlative label fixed on screen, then six or seven short deadpan hype lines read off the screenshot in sports-commentary rhythm, ending on a one-word verdict.
- **Mechanism:** Treating a mundane document with the language of a highlight reel. The lines are short enough that each lands as its own beat, and the screenshot lets the viewer check every claim.
- **Observed evidence:** 2.0M views on a 19.3K-follower account, 62.5x its 32K median, 16s, zero cuts, faint background music. Label stays on screen for the full video. The reference shows a private individual's profile and uses mild profanity.

- **What is borrowed vs new:** Borrowed: superlative label, rapid deadpan hype over a public screenshot. New: the subject is a small business's bio shown with permission and praised sincerely, no private person is mocked, the language is clean, and the turn to his own bio is new.

- Footage reviewed: yes · Not available: likes: not returned by the index, comments: not returned by the index, shares: not returned by the index, The reference's spoken result figure (over 500,000) conflicts with its on-screen slide (373 K), so its campaign numbers are not relied on here

### Why the reference worked
- **Observed hook:** "This chocolate brand literally sold chocolate bars with a piece missing on purpose."
- **First frame:** Stacked split screen. Top: hands tearing open a chocolate wrapper to show a bar with a corner missing, under a white header box reading "This is WILD marketing 🤯". Bottom: the presenter in a white shirt with a clip-on mic against a dark background.
- **Structure:** Fixed headline for the whole video. A brand does something that looks like a mistake, the public gets angry, 'then the twist hit', a result number, and a question to the audience at the end.
- **Mechanism:** The top panel shows the 'mistake' as proof while the bottom panel tells the story, so the viewer never has to take the claim on trust. The mistake-anger-twist order is a three-act story in under 45 seconds.
- **Observed evidence:** 2.7M views on a 3.6K-follower account, 433.8x its 6.2K median, 41s. Fixed header, word-by-word subtitles, campaign footage on top. Caption ends with a question to the viewer.
- **Hypothesis (not observed):** 'on purpose' plus visible evidence makes people watch for the twist, and the closing question produces comments naming a person or an opinion. A tiny account reaching 2.7M suggests the structure does the work.
- **Why it fits Alex:** He can be both the brand and the narrator: he runs the stunt on his own Trial Reel and reports the real result. That removes any unverified brand claim, shows a marketing experiment with his own numbers, and uses the Trial Reel workflow he already runs daily.
- **What is borrowed vs new:** Borrowed: stacked split screen, fixed headline, mistake then reaction then twist then result then question. New: the campaign is his own deliberate typo, the evidence is his own comment section, and the result is whatever his Insights show, including a failure.

### Alex's version
- **Concept:** Step one, before this video: he posts any silent Trial Reel from this package with 'Florida' spelled 'Flordia' in the on-screen text. Step two, 48 hours later: this split-screen video shows that Reel and its comments on top while he explains below that the typo was planted, and reports honestly whether corrections raised the comment count against his recent average.
- **Audience:** Creators and marketers curious about engagement tactics; viewers who enjoy being let in on a trick. · **Emotion:** Surprise at being shown the trick, then curiosity about the number
- **First frame:** Split screen. Top: his own Reel paused on the frame where the text reads 'Flordia', with a red circle around the word. Header: 'I did this on purpose'. Bottom: Alex chest-up with a clip-on mic.
- **First spoken line:** Two days ago I posted a video where I spelled Florida wrong. On purpose.

- **Structure:** Single uncut green-screen take, 2 minutes 7 seconds. Verdict first, then three itemized surprises in order (insurance, a $1,000 oil change, a $1,500 key), each told as a short scene with quoted dialogue, then a rating: "0 out of 10, do not recommend."

## V033 · "A good video works everywhere"? Same video: 2.8M on Instagram, 1,690 on YouTube

**Rank 46 of 50 · Score 67/100 · Day 5 · Batch E: Green screen and screen breakdowns · Marketing experiments and case studies · 33s**

### Reference
- YouTube Shorts · John Casterline · https://www.youtube.com/shorts/_dvhmNu7KUk
- Posted 2026-10-11 · 5.0M views · 3.1x the creator's median · creator had 8,500,000 followers · checked 2026-11-16 (vidIQ outlier index)
- Footage reviewed: yes · Not available: likes: not returned by the index, comments: not returned by the index, shares: not returned by the index, creator_median_views: the YouTube index returns only a breakout multiple, The channel has 8.5M subscribers and the breakout is 3.1x, so creator size explains much of the view count

### Why the reference worked
- **Observed hook:** "So a lot of people are speculating that MrBeast has been selling ad space in his thumbnails..."
- **First frame:** A young man in a white t-shirt standing behind a large black studio microphone in a dim room with purple and red light behind him.
- **Structure:** States what 'a lot of people' believe, shows the evidence as image overlays, pivots at 0:17 with a direct disagreement, gives one insider reason from his own craft, and closes by admitting he could be wrong.
- **Mechanism:** Opening on a belief the audience already holds, then contradicting it with a reason only a practitioner would know. The admission of uncertainty at the end invites argument instead of shutting it down.
- **Observed evidence:** 5.0M views, 31s, zero cuts, image overlays at four points, no CTA. Breakout only 3.1x on an 8.5M-subscriber channel, so this is the weakest evidence in the marketing set for the format itself.
- **Hypothesis (not observed):** viewers who hold the belief stay to see it challenged, and the hedge at the end gives both sides something to comment. The format works when the speaker has standing; for a small account the standing has to come from showing his own numbers.
- **Why it fits Alex:** He has a rare, checkable pair of numbers: the same client skit at 2.8M plays on Instagram and 1,690 views on YouTube, and Central Park at 13.5M on Instagram against 1.5M on TikTok. That is first-hand evidence against a belief most creators hold.
- **What is borrowed vs new:** Borrowed: 'a lot of people think X', evidence overlays, 'I don't think so', one insider reason, an honest hedge. New: the myth, the evidence (his own analytics screens), and every line. No other creator is discussed.

### Alex's version
- **Concept:** Alex takes on the belief that a good video performs anywhere you post it. He shows his own two cases as screenshots, says what he thinks it means for anyone judging their content by one platform, and admits the sample is two videos.
- **Audience:** Creators and business owners who deleted or gave up on a video because it flopped on one platform. · **Emotion:** Relief, plus the urge to argue
- **First frame:** Alex chest-up to camera at his desk, a screenshot of his '2 Types of Clients' Reel showing 2.8M floating beside his head.
- **First spoken line:** A lot of people think if a video is good, it'll work wherever you post it.

- **Brand fit:** 3 of 3 · **Read at:** 24h and 48h · **Primary:** Follows per 1000 non-follower accounts reached
