## V034 · Day N of the road to 100K: the daily public ledger

**Rank 27 of 50 · Score 77/100 · Day 5 · Batch G: Voiceover + b-roll and straight-to-camera · Personal growth and comeback series · 30s**

### Reference
- TikTok · @_mirrsyyy · https://www.tiktok.com/@_mirrsyyy/video/7691310658817707278
- Posted 2026-09-30 · 1.2M views · 671.5x the creator's median · creator had 11,300 followers · checked 2026-11-16 (vidIQ outlier index)
- Footage reviewed: yes · Not available: likes: not returned by the index, comments: not returned by the index, shares: not returned by the index, the on-screen payment date in the video (August 3, 2026) conflicts with the indexed post date; the index date is recorded

### Why the reference worked
- **Observed hook:** "Today is day 21 of paying off $55,000 in debt until I'm absolutely debt-free."
- **First frame:** Close-up selfie of a man in a black baseball cap and over-ear protective earmuffs, grey T-shirt, standing outdoors in front of a red brick building under an overcast sky.
- **Structure:** Day number and the total goal in the first sentence. The same short identity recap every episode (job, family, side work). Yesterday's results converted into today's action, with screenshots as proof. A small surprise. A dated mini-goal. An end card with the remaining balance. About 50 seconds, word-by-word captions, a direct ask for engagement at the end.
- **Mechanism:** The first sentence is identical every day except the number, so returning viewers recognise it within one second and new viewers get the whole premise at once. Yesterday's audience response changes today's number, which gives viewers a reason to believe their watching matters.
- **Observed evidence:** 1.2M views against a 1.8K median (671.5x) on an 11.3K-follower account. It is day 21, so 20 earlier episodes existed. The first sentence states the day count and the total. Screenshots appear on screen as receipts. The caption says "I'm proud of what we have achieved in just 21 days".
- **Hypothesis (not observed):** the daily repetition is what produced the outlier. By day 21 the account had a small returning audience that watched in the first hour, and one episode with an unusual result broke out. Viewers follow to see the balance reach zero. The explicit engagement ask in the reference is not needed for that and is left out.
- **Why it fits Alex:** He already has a public number (42,700), a target (100,000) and a date (Dec 31). He posts 4 to 10 Trial Reels daily, so there is a new result to report every morning. A daily ledger gives new viewers the reason to follow that his meme Reels lack, and it documents the rebuild after months of not posting.
- **What is borrowed vs new:** Borrowed: day count plus total goal in the first sentence, a fixed identity recap, yesterday's numbers shown with screenshots, a remaining-balance end card. New: the subject is follower growth, no money appears, there is no engagement-bait ask, the spoken script never changes so only the on-screen numbers are edited daily, and the tone is dry and self-mocking.

### Alex's version
- **Concept:** A 30-second daily episode, same words every day. Alex states the day, who he is and why he is being 'punished', then points at four screenshots: yesterday's best reel, worst reel, followers gained and followers left, and whether the current pace beats the deadline.
- **Audience:** People who followed after one viral Reel, other creators, and anyone who likes a public countdown with a real chance of failure. · **Emotion:** Suspense with a side of schadenfreude
- **First frame:** Walking selfie outdoors in Florida morning light, Alex already talking. Pinned header at the top: "Day {{REAL: day number}} · road to 100K by Dec 31".
- **First spoken line:** Day {{REAL: day number}} of getting to a hundred thousand followers by December thirty-first.

**CTA:** Soft, spoken: "See you tomorrow." No like, comment or share request.

### Production
- **Location:** The same outdoor walking route near home every morning. · **People:** 1 · **Film:** 6 min · **Edit:** 15 min · **Difficulty:** easy · **Batchable:** no
- **Props:** Phone, Yesterday's Reel insights screenshots (best and worst), Profile screenshot with follower count
- **Wardrobe:** Whatever he is wearing that morning. Different each day so episodes are distinguishable on the grid.
- **Shot list:**
  1. One continuous 30-second walking selfie with a stop at 22s. Film it twice, keep the better one.
- **B-roll:** Insights screenshot of yesterday's best reel; Insights screenshot of yesterday's worst reel; Profile header screenshot
- **Editor:** Build one template and duplicate it daily. Only five things change: the day number in the header, two screenshots, the gained and to-go numbers, and the projected date. Projected date = today + (followers still needed ÷ average followers gained per day since Day 1). Show it even when it is far past the deadline. Never crop the screenshots so tightly that the Reel cannot be identified. No music, so the daily repetition does not depend on a track.

### Test plan
- **Variants:** Original adaptation; Alternate hook: "I need fifty-seven thousand followers in six weeks and yesterday I got {{REAL}}."; Alternate payoff: the conditional 'days late / I don't trust it' line
- **Brand fit:** 3 of 3 · **Read at:** 24h and 48h · **Primary:** Follows per 1000 non-follower accounts reached
- **Promotion rule:** Brand fit 3. Evaluate after 5 consecutive daily episodes, not one: continue if the 5-episode average of follows per 1,000 reached is above the account median, and promote the single best episode each week.
- **If it wins, make:** Weekly recap every Sunday: the same ledger for seven days, with the best and worst reel of the week / A 'deadline day' episode on Dec 31 that states the final number, hit or miss / A halfway episode that reads out the three most accurate and three most wrong predictions from the comments
- **Why this score:** The reference shows a small account breaking out on episode 21 of a daily numeric ledger, and Alex has the three ingredients it needs: a number, a target and a date. The spoken script is fixed, so the daily cost is one take and five graphic swaps. It is a spoken 30-second walking video, which is untested for him, and the reference's outlier arrived only after three weeks of repetition, so early episodes may be flat.
- **Uncertainties:** The ledger needs five real numbers on screen each day; the spoken script needs only the day number. None are income figures.; Evidence is one TikTok account; the reference's own on-screen date conflicts with its indexed date.; If daily gains are small, the projected date will look absurd. That is usable as comedy only if he keeps posting it.; Overlaps in subject with the Day-1 launch video and the silent tally series. Run the launch once, then choose between this and the silent tally after 5 episodes of each.

## V036 · The medium coffee exists so you don't buy it (decoy pricing, filmed on a real menu board)

**Rank 34 of 50 · Score 73/100 · Day 6 · Batch G: Voiceover + b-roll and straight-to-camera · Marketing experiments and case studies · 36s**

### Reference
- Instagram · @itsjayfoster · https://www.instagram.com/reel/Ddl1zxSqi0Y
- Posted 2026-09-25 · 5.0M views · 150.1x the creator's median · creator had 8,000 followers · checked 2026-11-16 (vidIQ outlier index)
- Footage reviewed: yes · Not available: likes: not returned by the index, comments: not returned by the index, shares: not returned by the index, The factual claims made in the reference video were not verified

### Why the reference worked
- **Observed hook:** "Did you know when Steve Jobs launched the iPad, he put a price on a giant screen behind him, and he left it there while he talked?"
- **First frame:** Archival-style shot of a presenter on a keynote stage against a dark purple backdrop, hands clasped, smiling.
- **Structure:** Question hook about an odd choice, then 'it was the wrong price, on purpose' at 0:08, then the story in order, then the principle named in plain words in the last third, then a follow line.
- **Mechanism:** A 'did you know X did a strange thing' question, followed inside ten seconds by 'on purpose'. The explanation is held back until the story is finished, so the viewer has to stay to learn why.
- **Observed evidence:** 5.0M views on an 8K-follower account, 150.1x the creator's 33.2K median. 52.8s. Word-by-word subtitles. About 16 cuts of archival and stock footage. The principle is stated only from 0:36.
- **Hypothesis (not observed):** 'on purpose' turns a trivia fact into a puzzle, and viewers who finish feel they learned a trick they can spot elsewhere, so they send it to a friend the next time they see a price board. The small account size suggests the format carried it, not the creator.
- **Why it fits Alex:** It shows marketing knowledge through one concrete example instead of tips, which the audit says is missing from his feed (zero of nine recent Reels show his work). Filming a real board himself keeps it honest and cheap, and the voiceover over b-roll plays to his delivery.
- **What is borrowed vs new:** Borrowed: question hook, 'on purpose' turn, principle named last. New: the subject is an ordinary coffee menu Alex films and does the arithmetic on, there is no archival footage, no founder story, and the script admits he cannot prove intent.

### Alex's version
- **Concept:** Alex stands at a real coffee shop menu board, does the subtraction between small, medium and large on camera, and shows that the medium is priced to make the large feel like the smart choice. He names the decoy effect only at the end and admits he cannot prove the shop meant it.
- **Audience:** People 18-34 who like 'now I can't unsee it' explanations; small-business owners who set their own prices. · **Emotion:** Curiosity, then the small satisfaction of catching a trick
- **First frame:** Tight handheld shot of a three-size coffee menu board, Alex's finger already pointing at the middle price.
- **First spoken line:** Did you know the medium at this coffee shop is on the menu so you won't buy it?

- **What is borrowed vs new:** Borrowed: opening sentence that is the entire story, scenic proof behind him, advice that he immediately takes back. - **Mechanism:** The header does the hook's job for the entire runtime: anyone joining at any second knows the stakes. The spoken part is specific facts with ages and counts, not feelings, and the phrase 'nobody posts this part' frames the video as the thing other accounts hide.

- **Why it fits Alex:** It gives a new viewer the three facts the current feed hides: he is 23, he runs a company that gets brands views, and he is restarting in public. His irony (a views agency owner who stopped posting) lets him keep the format honest without the heaviness of the original.
- **What is borrowed vs new:** Borrowed: pinned one-line header ending in a 'nobody posts this' frame, single-take walking selfie, short factual sentences, a turn, a resolute last line. New: every fact and line is his, the tone is self-mocking rather than raw, it runs 38 seconds instead of 63, there is no music swell, and it ends on a public bet with a date.

### Why the reference worked
- **Observed hook:** "Why's nobody working?" On-screen text for the full video: "Me when I go anywhere Monday - Friday from 9-5"
- **First frame:** A young man in a cream overshirt holds a bag of apples over a cart in a warehouse-store produce section, looking around confused.
- **Structure:** One persistent caption. Four everyday locations in 13 seconds, 3 cuts. An internal-monologue voiceover judges everyone else for being out during work hours, and the last line reveals he has the same excuse he denies them.
- **Mechanism:** The voiceover says a thought most people have had and never said, the side-eye acting sells it with sound off, and the button line turns the judgment back on the narrator.
- **Observed evidence:** 7.3M views, 48.1x a 151.7K median, 2.7M followers. 13 seconds, 3 cuts, 4 scenes, persistent caption, no CTA. Final line in the transcript: "I mean, I'm personally here because it's my day off. But what are they doing here?"
- **Hypothesis (not observed):** people who are out on weekdays (shift workers, remote workers, students) tag each other, and the hypocritical last line prompts 'this is me' comments. The large account explains part of the reach, so treat the multiple, not the raw views, as the signal.
- **Why it fits Alex:** The inner-monologue voice over silent side-eye acting is a character piece like his client skit (2.8M). Flipping the paranoia onto himself lets him say 'I'm 23 and I run a company' without it being a flex.
- **What is borrowed vs new:** Borrowed: persistent caption, four quick locations, internal-monologue voiceover, a button that undercuts the narrator. New: the point of view is reversed (he is the one being judged, in his own head), all lines are new, and the button is a loud fake business call in a grocery aisle.

- **First frame:** Alex in gym clothes holding a basket in a grocery aisle, catching the eye of someone off camera and looking away too fast. Caption on screen.

## V045 · Strangers test the marketing guy: any business word in five words or I do push-ups

**Rank 35 of 50 · Score 73/100 · Day 7 · Batch F: Out in public, street, collaborator · Street interviews and social formats · 12s**

### Reference
- Instagram · @joshmattb · https://www.instagram.com/reel/Deukd-xxYzT
- Posted 2026-10-20 · 1.9M views · 75x the creator's median · creator had 47,000 followers · checked 2026-11-16 (vidIQ outlier index)
- Footage reviewed: yes · Not available: likes: not returned by the index, comments: not returned by the index, shares: not returned by the index, duration: not returned by search, taken from the watch job

### Why the reference worked
- **Observed hook:** "I do stocks."
- **First frame:** Full shot inside a bright multi-level shopping mall. A man in a black hoodie with the hood up and glasses holds a shopping bag; a young interviewer in a cream T-shirt holds a small wireless mic toward him. "I DO STOCKS." is on screen in bold white.
- **Structure:** One continuous take, 6 seconds, 0 cuts. The stranger states his field. The interviewer claims to share it and asks for one basic term from that field. The stranger gives a confident wrong answer. Hard cut on the answer.
- **Mechanism:** The entire video is a claim and an immediate test of the claim. The viewer hears the question, forms their own answer, and gets the outcome within 2 seconds. Cutting on the answer leaves no reaction to watch, so the loop restarts before the viewer has finished processing it.
- **Observed evidence:** 1.9M views against a 24.8K median (75x) on a 47K account. 6.3 seconds, single take, five caption lines. The research index logged three more entries by the same creator in the same format, so it is a repeated series for him.
- **Hypothesis (not observed):** viewers tag a friend who claims the same expertise, and people in that field comment the correct answer. The 6-second length means most views include a replay.
- **Why it fits Alex:** The length and single-take form match his 13.5M and 3.6M Reels. The reference gets its laugh at a stranger's expense, which is a consent and reputation risk for an agency owner, so the test is turned on Alex. Being quizzed on marketing terms shows his job in the first second and lets him use timing and physical comedy when he fails.
- **What is borrowed vs new:** Borrowed: claim-then-instant-test structure, single take, hard cut on the answer, sub-12-second length. New: the expert being tested is Alex, the stranger chooses the word, the answer has a five-word limit he counts on his fingers, and a failed answer is paid for with push-ups on the spot. The stranger is never the punchline.

### Alex's version
- **Concept:** Alex tells a stranger he runs a marketing agency and dares them to give him any business word. He must explain it in exactly five words, counted on his fingers. If he goes over or blanks, he drops and does push-ups where he stands.
- **Audience:** Marketers, business students, small business owners, and people who enjoy watching a confident person get tested. · **Emotion:** Playful tension
- **First frame:** Medium two-shot outdoors. Alex holds a small mic toward a stranger, already mid-sentence. Banner: "I run a marketing agency. Strangers test me."
- **First spoken line:** I run a marketing agency. Give me any business word.

| Time | Speaker | Dialogue | Action and acting | Camera | On-screen text | Edit | Audio |
|---|---|---|---|---|---|---|---|
| 0-3s | Alex | I run a marketing agency. Give me any business word. Five words or I do push-ups. | Confident, quick, holding up five fingers on "five words". Slight grin, like he has never lost. | Static two-shot from a tripod or collaborator at chest height, 3 meters back. | I run a marketing agency. Strangers test me. (banner, stays) | Start on the first syllable. Captions for every word. | Natural sound, lav mic on Alex, handheld mic toward stranger. |
| 3-5s | Stranger (real, unscripted) | [unscripted word] | Alex repeats the word once under his breath, eyes up and to the side, thinking. | Same shot, no cut. | Their word, large, center screen | Pop the word on screen the instant they say it. | Natural sound. |
| 5-9s | Alex | [live five-word answer, counted on fingers; prepared answers for likely words are in editor_instructions] | Counts each word on one hand, thumb first, held up to the lens. If he lands exactly five: freezes with the open hand up, smug. If he runs out of fingers or stalls for more than two seconds: his face drops. | Same shot. | A counter 1 to 5 ticking under his hand | Each word gets its own caption beat. | A soft tick per word. |
| 9-12s | Alex | PASS branch: "Five. Next."  FAIL branch: "...That's six." | PASS: closes the hand into a fist and points off-screen for the next person. FAIL: hands the mic to the stranger without a word and drops to push-ups on the pavement; the stranger is left holding the mic. | Same shot. On FAIL, tilt down to follow him. | PASS: ✅ 5/5   FAIL: ❌ push-ups owed: 10 | Cut hard on "Next" or on his first push-up. | PASS: one ding. FAIL: a buzzer, then just his breathing. |

**Alternate hooks**
1. "I get brands a million views for a living. Try to stump me with one word."
2. Open cold on the stranger saying their word, then Alex's face, with the banner explaining the rule
3. "Ask me any marketing word. If I need more than five words, I owe you ten push-ups."

**Alternate endings**
1. On a pass, the stranger immediately says a second, harder word and the video cuts on Alex's face before he answers.
2. On a fail, the stranger counts the push-ups out loud into the mic, cut at "three".

**Caption:** Running record in the comments. Current streak: {{REAL: his actual pass count}}.

**CTA:** None spoken. The series is the reason to follow.

### Production
- **Location:** Outside a coworking space, a business school, or a downtown lunch area on a weekday, where people are likely to know business words. · **People:** 2 · **Film:** 60 min · **Edit:** 25 min · **Difficulty:** medium · **Batchable:** yes
- **Props:** Small handheld wireless mic, Lav mic for Alex, Tripod or collaborator
- **Wardrobe:** T-shirt and shorts or pants he can do push-ups in.
- **Shot list:**
  1. Static two-shot: the dare, their word, his answer, pass or fail (repeat with 15 people)
  2. Tilt-down coverage of push-ups for every fail
  3. One wide of the location for a cover frame
- **Editor:** Before each interview say on camera: "I'm filming for my Instagram, are you okay with me posting this?" Keep that clip in your files as the consent record; it does not go in the edit. If they say no or hesitate, thank them and delete the clip. Ask 15 people in one session; each exchange is its own Reel. Usable = the stranger gave a real word unprompted and agreed to be posted. Post the fails as readily as the passes; a fail is the stronger video. Never tell the stranger what word to say and never re-shoot an answer. Prepared five-word answers Alex should rehearse so passes are real: ROI: "Money back versus money spent." SEO: "Getting Google to like you." Brand: "What they say without you." Algorithm: "A robot guessing your attention." Funnel: "Strangers in, customers out, hopefully." Engagement: "People doing anything but scrolling." Viral: "Strangers sharing it for free." KPI: "The number your boss watches." Conversion: "Looker becomes buyer. That's it." Niche: "Fewer people, more money, allegedly." Synergy: "Nobody knows. Meeting word. Next." Inflation: "That's economics. I do Reels." Fallback if fewer than three people play after 30 minutes: Alex films the same game with one willing person who gives five words in a row, cut as a 20-second gauntlet.

### Test plan
- **Variants:** Original adaptation; Alternate hook: opening cold on the stranger's word; Alternate payoff: the stranger counting his push-ups into the mic
- **Brand fit:** 3 of 3 · **Read at:** 24h and 48h · **Primary:** Follows per 1000 non-follower accounts reached
- **Promotion rule:** Brand fit 3. Promote at 48h if views reach 3x the trailing median and either follows or shares per 1,000 reached is top quartile; post one pass and one fail as separate trials on the same day to see which the audience prefers.
- **If it wins, make:** Same game inside a client's gym: members give him fitness-business words / Reverse round: he gives strangers one marketing word and they get five words, he does the push-ups if they succeed / Hard mode: three words instead of five
- **Why this score:** The reference proves a 6-second claim-and-test exchange can reach 1.9M from a 47K account, and it is a series for that creator. Turning the test on Alex removes the consent problem and puts "I run a marketing agency" in the first second of every episode. His acting carries the fail branch.
- **Uncertainties:** His answer is live and cannot be fully scripted; only the dare, the rule and the pass or fail lines are fixed.; The reference's humor came from a stranger being wrong. A version where the host is tested is a different comedic engine and has no direct view evidence.; Depends on strangers offering interesting words; 'money' or 'sales' make dull episodes.; Needs a collaborator or a tripod in a public place.

### Why the reference worked
- **Observed hook:** "Y'all think I can start an entire clothing brand before my train ends?"
- **First frame:** Low-angle selfie of a young man outside beside a passenger train, holding a small wireless mic with a fuzzy windscreen.
- **Structure:** A business task bounded by a real-world clock, an on-screen countdown, three labelled steps (STEP 1/3, 2/3, 3/3), narration that names its own story beats, and a 'follow to see if I make any money' close.
- **Mechanism:** The deadline is physical and outside the creator's control, so the viewer can judge progress against the clock. The three-step counter tells the viewer how much is left, which holds them through a long video.
- **Observed evidence:** 6.1M views, 21.1x the creator's 286.9K median, 129.9s, about 37 cuts. Countdown timestamps on screen throughout. Step cards. A follow CTA tied to an unresolved outcome.
- **Hypothesis (not observed):** people stay because a visible clock plus 'step 2 of 3' makes quitting feel like leaving before the result, and the open outcome gives a concrete reason to follow. Comments are likely predictions and advice.
- **Why it fits Alex:** His only YouTube breakout was a real challenge with an outcome (saxophone in one day, 30x his median). Pitching a business is his actual job, so the challenge demonstrates the agency without a pitch. Florida heat gives him a clock nobody else has.
- **What is borrowed vs new:** Borrowed: a task raced against a physical clock, three on-screen steps, an honest open result. New: the clock is melting ice in Florida, the task is landing a yes from a real business for a free video, the narration is his, and the video is cut to about a minute.

### Alex's version
- **Concept:** Alex buys an iced coffee, sets it on the hood of the Lexus in the Florida sun, and has until the ice is gone to walk a strip of shops and get one owner to agree to let him make them a free video. Real attempts, real answers, and the result is shown either way.

- **Location:** A walkable strip of independent shops in his Florida area (gym, smoothie bar, barber, boutique), Lexus parked in sun nearby · **People:** 1 · **Film:** 60 min · **Edit:** 75 min · **Difficulty:** hard · **Batchable:** no

Keep to five doors maximum so it stays under an hour. Use the real answers as the on-screen 'door N' text in four words or fewer. Use only the true outcome branch in rows 46-60 and delete the other. The ice timer is a simple shrinking icon, not a fake clock. If a business says yes, the Part 2 video must actually get made.

- **Brand fit:** 3 of 3 · **Read at:** 24h and 48h · **Primary:** Follows per 1000 non-follower accounts reached
- **Promotion rule:** Brand fit 3: promote at 48h if views are at least 3x his trailing median, and because this is a two-part series, promote it regardless of views if follows per 1,000 reached is at least 2x his median.
- **If it wins, make:** Part 2: the free video he made for the business that said yes, with the owner's reaction / Same challenge at gyms only, since gyms are a niche he already works in / Reverse version: he has until the ice melts to get a stranger to explain what they think he does for work
- **Why this score:** Highest follow motivation in the marketing pillar: it shows his job, his age, his move, and leaves an open result. It is also the hardest shoot here: public filming, real rejection, and a 60-second edit, and the outcome is out of his control.
- **Uncertainties:** Outcome is unknown; the script has lines for both results and only the true one may be used; Owners may refuse to be filmed; the plan covers that with face-on-Alex framing; A 60-second multi-cut video is a new length for him; Ice may melt faster or slower than the walk; that is the point, do not fake it; The interviewee lines cannot be scripted

## V047 · Doing the math out loud with a local business owner, then asking how many customers come from Instagram

**Rank 47 of 50 · Score 64/100 · Day 7 · Batch F: Out in public, street, collaborator · Street interviews and social formats · 40s**

### Reference
- Instagram · @lunarosendorff · https://www.instagram.com/reel/DdEUNEXtAbw
- Posted 2026-09-16 · 1.8M views · 2246.7x the creator's median · creator had 6,500 followers · checked 2026-11-16 (vidIQ outlier index)
- Footage reviewed: yes · Not available: likes: not returned by the index, comments: not returned by the index, shares: not returned by the index, duration: not returned by search, taken from the watch job

### Why the reference worked
- **Observed hook:** "How much do you charge to walk a dog?"
- **First frame:** Close-up walking selfie of a woman with long blonde hair and dark sunglasses on a paved, tree-lined trail; beside her a man in a bright yellow T-shirt holds several dog leashes.
- **Structure:** A price question as the first words. A one-number answer. The creator multiplies it out loud, in real time, with the worker correcting her inputs, until an hourly figure lands. A static text overlay states the surprising conclusion for the first 6 seconds. Single take, 0 cuts, 120 seconds.
- **Mechanism:** The viewer does the multiplication alongside the creator, so the surprise is something they compute rather than something they are told. The price question needs no setup, and the overlay tells sound-off viewers the payoff is a number worth waiting for.
- **Observed evidence:** 1.8M views against an 803 median (2,246.7x) on a 6.5K-follower account, the largest multiple in the street group. The first spoken words are the price question. The overlay reads "Dog walking is lucrative AF / like $300K a year, actually"; that annual figure is the creator's extrapolation and is not stated by the worker. The video is 120 seconds and loose after the first 30.
- **Hypothesis (not observed):** people send it to a friend with "we're in the wrong job" and argue about the math in the comments (hours, costs, taxes). The comment disputes extend distribution. The first 30 seconds hold nearly all the value.
- **Why it fits Alex:** An agency owner asking a local business about unit price and daily volume is in character, and the follow-up question, how many of those customers come from Instagram, turns a curiosity video into a demonstration of what he does. Keeping it under 45 seconds removes the slack in the reference.
- **What is borrowed vs new:** Borrowed: price question as the first words, arithmetic done out loud with the owner, a number as the landing point. New: the subject is a Florida small business of Alex's choosing, the math stops at a per-day figure the owner has confirmed (no extrapolated annual income), a second question about social media is added, the take is cut to 40 seconds, and the on-screen text states only what was actually said.

### Alex's version
- **Concept:** Alex asks a local owner-operator (barber, food truck, car detailer, coffee cart) what one unit costs and how many they do a day, multiplies it out loud with them, and then asks how many of those customers found them on Instagram. The answer to the second question is the ending.
- **Audience:** Small business owners, people curious about what local businesses make per day, and marketers. · **Emotion:** Curiosity, then a small jolt

### Production
- **Location:** An owner-operated local business in Florida where the owner is present and the product or service has one clear unit price: barber shop, food truck, mobile detailer, coffee cart, market stall. · **People:** 2 · **Film:** 75 min · **Edit:** 40 min · **Difficulty:** hard · **Batchable:** yes
- **Props:** Phone, Two lav mics or one lav plus a handheld, Small tripod (optional)
- **Wardrobe:** Casual and clean. Nothing with the agency name on it, so it does not look like a sales call.
- **Shot list:**

  2. A 3-second insert of the product or service being done, for a cover frame
  3. A 2-second exterior of the business with its sign, only if the owner wants the name shown

Approach 5 businesses in a session and buy something at each. Usable = owner on camera, a single unit price, a daily volume they state themselves, and consent to post the numbers. Expect 1 to 2 usable out of 5. Cut the raw take to 40 seconds with jump cuts; keep every number the owner says in their own voice. On-screen math must use only figures spoken in the video and must carry the label "before costs, their numbers". Do not multiply to a monthly or yearly income and do not put any income claim in the hook text. Fallback if no owner agrees to share numbers: ask only the Instagram question ("Out of ten customers, how many found you on Instagram?") to five owners and cut a 20-second supercut of the five answers.

- **Promotion rule:** Brand fit 3. Promote at 48h if views reach 3x the trailing median and follows per 1,000 reached is top quartile; if a business owner inquiry arrives from it, log that as a qualified lead regardless of views.
- **If it wins, make:** Same three questions asked at a gym: price of one membership, sign-ups per week, how many came from Instagram / Follow-up visit two weeks later after filming one free Reel for that owner, with the new Instagram answer / Five owners, one question: "What's the one post that actually brought someone in?"
- **Why this score:** The reference is a 2,246x outlier from a 6.5K account, so the format carried it, and the first 30 seconds contain the whole mechanism. Adding the Instagram question makes the same structure demonstrate Alex's job and can produce real business inquiries. It is the most logistically demanding of the street scripts.

## V048 · 60 seconds to guess what a stranger does for work, yes-or-no questions only

**Rank 48 of 50 · Score 59/100 · Day 7 · Batch F: Out in public, street, collaborator · Street interviews and social formats · 50s**

### Reference
- YouTube Shorts · Max Klymenko · https://www.youtube.com/shorts/Yllerhv7A60
- Posted 2026-09-04 · 10.6M views · 4.1x the creator's median · creator had 4,800,000 followers · checked 2026-11-16 (vidIQ outlier index)
- Footage reviewed: yes · Not available: likes: not returned by the index, comments: not returned by the index, shares: not returned by the index, creator_median_views: the YouTube tool returns a breakout multiple only, outlier_multiple is the tool's breakout score (4.1), not views divided by median, URL built from the video ID the tool returned

### Why the reference worked
- **Observed hook:** "Less than a hundred people in the world do what I do, to have me you have to have me on the ladder."
- **First frame:** Medium close-up of a woman with dark hair, sunglasses and gold hoop earrings in a burgundy tank top, hands on hips on a sunny street, talking animatedly. Yellow subtitles read "LESS THAN / 100 PEOPLE".
- **Structure:** Cold open on the guest's intriguing clue about her own job. Name and where she is from. The rule: the host has 2 minutes to guess the job. A visible countdown. Rapid yes-or-no and lateral questions with meme inserts. The timer hits zero, a banner declares the host did not guess, and the guest reveals the job to camera with proof footage. 143 seconds, about 47 cuts, two camera angles, a jingle.
- **Mechanism:** The viewer plays along. Each answer narrows the possibilities, the countdown sets a deadline, and the opening clue ("less than a hundred people") promises the answer will be worth it. The reveal is withheld to the last seconds, so leaving early means not finding out.
- **Observed evidence:** 10.6M views, the highest in the street group, on a 4.8M-subscriber channel with a breakout score of only 4.1. 143 seconds. A 2-minute on-screen countdown. The host fails to guess; the answer is "My partner and I run an analog photo booth company in Australia." A proof clip follows the reveal.
- **Hypothesis (not observed):** comments fill with viewers' own guesses and with the timestamp where they worked it out, and people rewatch to see which clue should have given it away. Much of the view count is explained by the channel's size, so the format evidence is weaker than the raw number.
- **Why it fits Alex:** It is a performance format: thinking out loud under a clock is where an actor is entertaining. The reveal can be flipped at the end so the stranger gets 10 seconds to guess his job, which puts "I get brands a million views" on screen. The reference's ladder, jingle and two-camera setup are not needed for the mechanism.
- **What is borrowed vs new:** Borrowed: guess-the-job game, visible countdown, yes-or-no questioning, reveal at the end. New: 60 seconds instead of 120, one phone, no ladder, no jingle, no meme inserts, an original fixed question list, and a 10-second reverse round where the stranger guesses what Alex does.

### Alex's version
- **Concept:** Alex gets 60 seconds of yes-or-no questions to guess what a stranger does for work, with a countdown on screen. Win or lose, the stranger reveals the answer, then gets 10 seconds to guess his job. Edited to about 50 seconds.
- **Audience:** Game and street-interview viewers; local Florida audience; anyone who plays along in the comments. · **Emotion:** Play-along suspense
- **First frame:** Medium shot of the stranger alone, mid-sentence, giving their one clue. Countdown clock reading 60 in the top corner, not yet running. Text: "60 seconds to guess their job".

**Caption:** Record so far: {{REAL: wins}} for {{REAL: attempts}}. Did you get it before I did?

**CTA:** Caption question only. No spoken ask.

### Production
- **Location:** Public place with people who are not rushing: a park, pier, farmers market or outdoor cafe strip in Florida. · **People:** 2 · **Film:** 90 min · **Edit:** 70 min · **Difficulty:** hard · **Batchable:** yes
- **Props:** Phone on a tripod or with a collaborator, Handheld wireless mic or two lavs, A 60-second timer running on a second phone for Alex to see
- **Wardrobe:** Same outfit every episode of the series so it is recognisable.
- **Shot list:**
  1. Stranger alone: the one-sentence clue
  2. Two-shot: rules and clap
  3. Two-shot: the full 60-second question round, uncut in camera
  4. Reveal and Alex's reaction
  5. Reverse round: stranger guesses his job
  6. Insert: stranger's phone showing proof of their work, if offered
- **B-roll:** Wide of the location for the cover frame
- **Editor:** Before each interview say on camera: "I'm filming for my Instagram, are you okay with me posting this?" Keep that clip in your files as the consent record; it does not go in the edit. If they say no or hesitate, thank them and delete the clip. Explain the game before rolling and ask them not to say their job. Play 6 to 8 rounds in a session; post only rounds where the job is interesting to hear, and post losses as readily as wins. The questions are fixed and asked in this order so episodes are comparable; if an answer makes a later question pointless, skip it and go to the next. His final guess is always live. Never learn the job in advance and never re-shoot a guess. The on-screen countdown must reflect real elapsed time in the round even though pauses are cut: place the timer values at the true time each question was asked. Keep the win or loss record honest and cumulative. Fallback if nobody has a guessable job after 8 rounds: cut the three funniest wrong guesses into a 25-second reel titled "Three jobs I got very wrong today".

### Test plan
- **Variants:** Original adaptation; Alternate hook: opening on the wrong guess and buzzer, then '60 seconds earlier'; Alternate payoff: no reverse round, ending on the reveal banner
- **Brand fit:** 2 of 3 · **Read at:** 24h and 48h · **Primary:** Follows per 1000 non-follower accounts reached
- **Promotion rule:** Brand fit 2. Promote at 48h if views reach 3x the trailing median and comments per 1,000 reached is the highest of that day's batch, since the format is built to collect guesses; make three episodes before applying the kill rule.
- **If it wins, make:** Business-owner edition: guess what the shop sells from outside, 60 seconds, owner answers yes or no / Reverse-only edition: strangers get 30 seconds of yes-or-no questions to guess what Alex does / Client edition: he guesses a gym member's day job between sets
- **Why this score:** The play-along guessing game with a visible clock is a durable structure and suits a performer. The simplification to one phone and 60 seconds keeps the mechanism and removes the production cost. The reference's 10.6M views come from a 4.8M-subscriber channel at only a 4.1 breakout score, so the number says more about the channel than the format.
- **Uncertainties:** All stranger lines and Alex's final guess are unscripted.; Weakest format evidence in the street group despite the highest view count.; Evidence is a 143-second YouTube Short; a 50-second Instagram cut is an untested compression.; Needs a collaborator and a location where people have a few minutes.; 'Is it legal in all fifty states?' is a joke question; drop it if the person seems uncomfortable.

## V049 · I just moved to Florida: asking strangers what they do, with a proof cutaway

**Rank 49 of 50 · Score 56/100 · Day 7 · Batch F: Out in public, street, collaborator · Street interviews and social formats · 18s**

### Reference
- Instagram · @trwlsh · https://www.instagram.com/reel/Deh-3QtvNv2
- Posted 2026-10-15 · 3.2M views · 34.2x the creator's median · creator had 348,200 followers · checked 2026-11-16 (vidIQ outlier index)
- Footage reviewed: yes · Not available: likes: not returned by the index, comments: not returned by the index, shares: not returned by the index

### Why the reference worked
- **Observed hook:** "Yo, excuse me."
- **First frame:** Wide, low-angle shot on a busy New York sidewalk. A woman in a white ribbed tank top and sparkling light-blue jeans, holding a denim handbag and phone, walks toward the camera past parked black SUVs.
- **Structure:** Approach and compliment in one breath, straight into "what do you do for a living?" (0-2s). Unexpected answer (2-4s). A 2-second cutaway of the person doing that job, with her own handle overlaid (4-6s). Interviewer's disbelief (6-9s). A playful physical button from the interviewee (11-13s). 16 seconds, 2 cuts, burned-in captions.
- **Mechanism:** The question is answered by second 4, and the answer contradicts what the first frame led the viewer to expect. The cutaway then proves it before the viewer can doubt it. The whole arc of guess, surprise and proof finishes in 6 seconds, and the rest is charm.
- **Observed evidence:** 3.2M views against a 93.9K median (34.2x) on a 348K account. 16 seconds, 2 cuts. The answer "I'm a professional boxer" lands at 0:02 to 0:04. The proof cutaway carries the interviewee's own handle. The interviewer's lines total 21 words.
- **Hypothesis (not observed):** the share is driven by the mismatch between appearance and job, which viewers pass on with "did not expect that". The interviewee also reposts it because her handle and her work are shown, which adds her audience.
- **Why it fits Alex:** He is new to Florida, which is a true reason to be asking. It is 16 seconds and mostly reaction, which suits an actor. Street interviews are untested for him, so this is the smallest possible version to test: one question, one phone.
- **What is borrowed vs new:** Borrowed: compliment-then-question open, proof cutaway from the interviewee's own phone, a playful closing beat. New: the framing is that he just moved to Florida and is finding out what people here do; the closing beat is the stranger guessing his job; the compliment must be specific and true each time; nothing the stranger says is scripted.

### Alex's version
- **Concept:** Alex, new to Florida, stops people and asks what they do for work. Only answers that contradict first impressions make the cut. Each is proved with two seconds of footage from the person's own phone, then the stranger guesses what Alex does.
- **Audience:** Local Florida viewers, people who like street interviews, and whoever the interviewee tags. · **Emotion:** Surprise
- **First frame:** Low, wide shot on a busy sidewalk or outdoor plaza. A person is walking toward the camera. Banner at the top: "I just moved to Florida. Asking what people do here."
- **First spoken line:** Hey, sorry, quick one.

**CTA:** None spoken. Caption may add: "Part 2 tomorrow."

### Production
- **Location:** A walkable area with steady foot traffic near home: a downtown strip, a farmers market, or an outdoor shopping plaza. Avoid malls and private property without permission. · **People:** 2 · **Film:** 90 min · **Edit:** 35 min · **Difficulty:** hard · **Batchable:** yes
- **Props:** Phone with wide lens, Small wireless lav or handheld mic, A second phone or AirDrop ready to receive the stranger's proof clip
- **Wardrobe:** Clean, plain, approachable. No sunglasses, so people can see his eyes.

  5. Reveal and fist bump

- **Editor:** Before anything else, say on camera: "I'm filming for my Instagram, are you okay with me posting this?" and keep that clip in your files as the consent record; it does not go in the edit. Ask 15 to 20 people in one session. A usable answer meets all three: (1) the job is not what the first frame suggests, (2) they can show proof on their phone within 30 seconds, (3) they said yes to posting. Expect 2 to 4 usable interviews; each becomes its own Reel. Fallback if none qualify after 20: cut a 15-second supercut of five answers back to back with the banner "5 strangers, 5 jobs, Florida" and no proof cutaways. Never re-voice, reorder words within an answer, or stage a stranger. Blur any bystander who looks at the camera and did not consent.

### Test plan
- **Variants:** Original adaptation; Alternate hook: opening cold on the stranger's answer, then rewinding; Alternate payoff: "I make these," pointing at the camera
- **Brand fit:** 2 of 3 · **Read at:** 24h and 48h · **Primary:** Follows per 1000 non-follower accounts reached
- **Promotion rule:** Brand fit 2. Promote at 48h if views reach 3x the trailing median and shares per 1,000 reached is top quartile; this format is a reach test, so do not expect top-quartile follows on the first one.
- **If it wins, make:** Same question asked only to people working at local small businesses, with the proof cutaway being their storefront / Same format at a gym at 6 a.m.: "What do you do that you're up this early?" / A version where the stranger guesses his job first and he has to live with the worst guess as the on-screen title
- **Why this score:** The reference is a clean 16-second structure with the surprise delivered by second 4 and proof by second 6, and it is repeatable daily. The move to Florida gives Alex an honest premise. The outcome depends on who he happens to stop, and the reference account is large (348K), so the evidence that the format alone drives views is moderate.
- **Uncertainties:** The stranger's lines cannot be written; quality depends on finding a real mismatch between look and job.; The compliment token must be filled in live and must be sincere.; Filming people in public needs explicit consent on camera every time; some locations prohibit filming.; A collaborator makes the two-shot far better. Solo is possible with a wide lens but weaker.; Untested category for his account.

## V050 · Screen Time chain, episode N: five strangers, each reacts to the last person's number

**Rank 50 of 50 · Score 55/100 · Day 7 · Batch F: Out in public, street, collaborator · Street interviews and social formats · 56s**

### Reference
- TikTok · @yashixv · https://www.tiktok.com/@yashixv/video/7684093645712100621
- Posted 2026-09-11 · 4.0M views · 8.7x the creator's median · creator had 1,300,000 followers · checked 2026-11-16 (vidIQ outlier index)
- Footage reviewed: yes · Not available: likes: not returned by the index, comments: not returned by the index, shares: not returned by the index

### Why the reference worked
- **Observed hook:** "How much is in your bank account?"
- **First frame:** A man in a dark brown T-shirt with a backpack stands half-turned from the camera on a boardwalk near Redondo Landing, interviewing a woman walking past.
- **Structure:** Numbered episode with a location. Five strangers each reveal a number and their job. Before revealing their own, each new stranger is told the previous stranger's number and reacts to it. A five-icon tally at the bottom fills in as each person is added. Each number is shown as a card. About 93 seconds, roughly 20 cuts, a second person filming two-shots.
- **Mechanism:** Every stranger is both a judge and a contestant. The viewer hears an opinion about a number, then immediately gets to compare the judge's own number against it. The tally shows how many reveals are left, which holds viewers to the fifth.
- **Observed evidence:** 4.0M views against a 461.9K median (8.7x) on a 1.3M-follower account. Captioned as a numbered episode. The cards carry the line "Screenshot for illustrative purposes. Numbers are verified by me." Five people, five cards. The interviewer's lines are re-voiced with text-to-speech.
- **Hypothesis (not observed):** the chain produces hypocrisy and humility moments (someone mocks a number, then reveals a worse one), and those are the clips viewers replay and quote in comments. The episode numbering produces follows because the format is identical every time.
- **Why it fits Alex:** The chain-and-tally structure does not depend on the number being money. Daily Screen Time is on every iPhone, is verifiable on camera, is not financially invasive, and is on-brand for someone who makes short-form video for a living. The final reveal can be his own number, which is the punchline an agency owner can deliver.
- **What is borrowed vs new:** Borrowed: numbered location episode, five people, react-to-the-previous-person chain, running five-slot tally. New: the number is today's Screen Time shown on the person's own phone, no bank balances or jobs-plus-money pairing, Alex speaks his own questions (no text-to-speech), the real phone screen is filmed instead of an illustrative card, and a sixth reveal, Alex's own Screen Time, closes the episode.

### Alex's version
- **Concept:** Episode-numbered street series. Alex asks five strangers for today's Screen Time. Each one first judges the previous person's number, then shows their own on their phone. A five-slot tally fills along the bottom. Last, the fifth stranger judges Alex's number.
- **Audience:** Everyone with a phone; skews 16 to 30. Location tag pulls local viewers. · **Emotion:** Judgment, then being caught
- **First frame:** Two-shot on a busy pier, boardwalk or downtown sidewalk. Alex holds a mic toward a stranger who is pulling their phone out. Title bar: "Screen Time · Episode {{REAL: number}} · {{REAL: location}}". Five empty circles along the bottom.
- **First spoken line:** What's your screen time today?

| Time | Speaker | Dialogue | Action and acting | Camera | On-screen text | Edit | Audio |
|---|---|---|---|---|---|---|---|
| 0-3s | Alex | What's your screen time today? Don't guess. Open it. | Quick, cheerful, pointing at their phone. Already knows this will hurt. | Two-shot filmed by a collaborator, waist up. | Screen Time · Episode {{REAL: number}} · {{REAL: location}} | Five empty circles at the bottom from frame 0. | Natural sound. Light percussion loop at low volume. |
| 3-8s | Stranger 1 (real, unscripted) | [unscripted; shows their Screen Time screen] | Alex leans in to read it, says the number out loud exactly as shown, then gives one beat of silent reaction scaled to the number. | Insert: close-up of their phone screen held to the lens, 1.5s. Back to two-shot. | Their number, large. Circle 1 fills with it. | Ding as the circle fills. | Ding. |
| 8-12s | Alex | Okay. The person before you had {{REAL: stranger 1's number}}. Thoughts? | New stranger. Alex asks it neutrally, giving nothing away. | Two-shot, new background a few meters along. | Last person: {{REAL: stranger 1's number}}. Thoughts? | Hard cut from the previous person. | Natural sound. |
| 12-17s | Stranger 2 (real, unscripted) | [unscripted judgment of the previous number] | Alex nods along, encouraging, like a lawyer letting a witness talk. | Hold on stranger 2. | Caption of their exact words | Keep their strongest sentence only. | Natural sound. |
| 17-22s | Alex | Great. Now open yours. | Says it with a small, slow smile. Holds the mic closer. | Two-shot, then insert of their phone screen. | Their number. Circle 2 fills. | Freeze 0.3s on their face as they see their own number. | Ding. |
| 22-31s | Alex + Stranger 3 (real, unscripted) | Alex: "Last person had {{REAL: stranger 2's number}}. Thoughts?" Stranger 3: [unscripted judgment]. Alex: "Now yours." Stranger 3: [shows screen] | Same pattern. Alex speeds up his delivery each round; the audience knows the game now. | Two-shot, insert, two-shot. | Circle 3 fills. | Tighter cuts than round 2. | Ding. |
| 31-40s | Alex + Stranger 4 (real, unscripted) | Alex: "Last person: {{REAL: stranger 3's number}}." Stranger 4: [unscripted judgment]. Alex: "Yours." Stranger 4: [shows screen] | Alex now only says the number and one word. Raises eyebrows at the lens if stranger 4's number is worse than the one they judged. | Two-shot, insert, two-shot. | Circle 4 fills. | Fastest round. | Ding. |
| 40-48s | Alex + Stranger 5 (real, unscripted) | Alex: "Last person: {{REAL: stranger 4's number}}." Stranger 5: [unscripted judgment]. Alex: "Yours." Stranger 5: [shows screen] | Same pattern. | Two-shot, insert, two-shot. | Circle 5 fills. All five visible. | Hold the full tally for one second. | Ding, then the percussion loop stops. |
| 48-53s | Alex | I make videos for a living, so be nice. This is mine. | Hands the mic to stranger 5. Pulls out his own phone, opens Screen Time, turns it to them first, then to the lens. Braces. | Two-shot, then insert of Alex's real Screen Time screen. | Alex (makes Reels for a living): {{REAL: his actual Screen Time today}} | A sixth circle appears, larger, and fills with his number. | Silence, then a low drum hit on the reveal. |
| 53-56s | Stranger 5 (real, unscripted) | [unscripted reaction to Alex's number] | Alex takes it. Nods slowly, puts the phone away, walks off. | Hold on stranger 5, Alex exits frame. | Caption of their exact words | Cut on his exit. | Natural sound, hard out. |

**Alternate hooks**
1. Open on stranger 2 judging the unseen number harshly, then cut to the title bar
2. "Guess the screen time of the last person I stopped."
3. Title bar only, first words from the stranger reading their own number in shock

**Alternate endings**
1. After his reveal, Alex asks stranger 5: "What should my limit be?" and the episode ends on their unscripted number, shown as "Episode goal".
2. End card: the six numbers ranked, Alex's highlighted, with "Episode {{REAL: next number}} tomorrow".

**Caption:** Episode {{REAL: number}}, {{REAL: location}}. I lost again.

**CTA:** Soft, on the end card only: next episode number.

### Production
- **Location:** A busy walkable public place in Florida: pier, boardwalk, downtown strip, campus edge. · **People:** 2 · **Film:** 90 min · **Edit:** 75 min · **Difficulty:** hard · **Batchable:** yes
- **Props:** Handheld wireless mic, Lav for Alex, Collaborator's phone on a gimbal or held steady, Alex's own phone for the final reveal
- **Wardrobe:** Same outfit for the whole episode. A recognisable cap or jacket helps the series read as a series.
- **Shot list:**
  1. Stranger 1: question and reveal with phone-screen insert
  2. Strangers 2 to 5: judgment of the previous number, then their own reveal with insert
  3. Alex's own Screen Time reveal to stranger 5 and their reaction
  4. Two wide establishing shots of the location for the title frame
- **B-roll:** Close inserts of each phone's Screen Time screen (shoot every one, they are the proof)
- **Editor:** Before each interview say on camera: "I'm filming for my Instagram, are you okay with me posting this?" Keep that clip in your files as the consent record; it does not go in the edit. If they say no or hesitate, thank them and delete the clip. Also ask each person: "Okay if I show your Screen Time number?" Film only the daily total, never the app list or notifications; if the app breakdown is visible, crop or blur it. Stop 12 to 15 people to get 5 who will open Screen Time on camera. The chain must be real: stranger N is told stranger N-1's actual number, in the order filmed. Do not reorder people to manufacture a better contrast. Build the tally as five circles plus one larger sixth for Alex. Round lengths shrink: about 5s, 9s, 9s, 9s, 8s. Alex's number must be his real Screen Time on the filming day. Fallback if fewer than five agree: publish a three-person episode with three circles; do not pad with staged participants. Android users can show Digital Wellbeing instead.

### Test plan
- **Variants:** Original adaptation; Alternate hook: opening on stranger 2's harsh judgment before the title; Alternate payoff: the ranked end card with next episode number
- **Brand fit:** 2 of 3 · **Read at:** 24h and 48h · **Primary:** Follows per 1000 non-follower accounts reached
- **Promotion rule:** Brand fit 2. Promote at 48h if views reach 3x the trailing median and shares per 1,000 reached is top quartile; make three episodes before judging follows, since the reference format converts through episode numbering.
- **If it wins, make:** Same chain with 'views on your best post ever', ending on Alex's 13.5M Reel / Same chain with follower counts at a creator meetup or campus / Same chain with 'unread notifications right now'
- **Why this score:** The react-to-the-previous-person chain is a strong structural idea that survives changing the number from bank balance to Screen Time, and the swap makes it verifiable on camera and safe to film. Ending on Alex's own number ties it to what he does. Evidence is weaker than the view count suggests: the reference is a 1.3M-follower account at only 8.7x its median, and money is a stronger hook than Screen Time.
- **Uncertainties:** Every stranger line is unscripted, and the best moments depend on someone judging a number and then revealing a worse one.; Longest script in the street group (56s) and the heaviest edit.; Needs a collaborator to film.; Bank balance to Screen Time lowers the stakes; test the 'best post views' variant if the first episode is flat.; Episode number, location and all six numbers are filled in from the real shoot.
